## Detailed Results

### first_letter_q_advanced
**Params:** 1st | Letter Q | advanced

#### Within: 94.4% (17/18 pairs)

**Review & Connect**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Sort the sound (voiced vs unvoiced; whisper “voice/air”) | high | Matches Form D Review & Connect activity “Sort the sound” (high): sorting sounds by voiced/unvoiced or continuous/stops. |
| 2 | Corrective feedback/discrimination practice: contrast /k/ vs /g/ with mouth cues + hand on throat | middle | Not a named Form D activity, but it functions like explicit sound discrimination and articulatory comparison to address a likely confusion (less demanding than generating/writing graphemes; more demanding than simple choral production). |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Sort the sound (voiced vs unvoiced; whisper “voice/air”) | Corrective feedback/discrimination practice: contrast /k/ vs /g/ with mouth cues + hand on throat | + | Although this goes from high (Sort the sound) to a lower-demand feedback routine, it matches Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #2 (Corrective Feedback After Assessment/Performance). The teacher is responding to errors from the sort and tightening /k/–/g/ discrimination before moving on. |

**Model, Explain & Engage**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce target spelling pattern qu for /k/ + gesture practice (choral /k/) | low | Primarily choral production with a gesture; closest to Form D “Sound Spark”/“Tap that Sound” type work (low) because students are producing/identifying a known phoneme rather than manipulating it. |
| 2 | Articulation compare/contrast /k/ vs /g/ with partner talk + hand-on-throat check | middle | Requires analyzing and explaining a contrast (voicing) and using partner discussion; comparable in cognitive demand to discrimination-focused activities (around Form D middle such as tasks that require attending to sound features/positions). |
| 3 | Sound Switch-a-Roo with /k/ spelled c/k/qu (identify which spelling fits in spoken words) | high | Direct match to Form D Model/Explain/Engage “Sound Switch-a-Roo” (high): substituting/choosing sound-spelling in words, requiring phoneme-grapheme mapping and flexible switching. |
| 4 | Sound Puzzle to build words with qu + other phonemes (blend to make words) | high | Direct match to Form D “Sound Puzzle” (high): combine given phonemes including target to form/blend words. |
| 5 | Teacher modeling of uppercase Q and lowercase q formation with verbal stroke cues (students watch, count strokes, answer what’s same/different) | low | This is explicit teacher modeling with observation and simple recall questions; aligns with the lowest-demand phase before students attempt writing (akin to low-level rehearsal/modeling preceding guided writing). |
| 6 | Corrective feedback: distinguish lowercase q vs p (teacher comparison + partner talk; students fix one q) | middle | Error analysis of confusable letterforms and making a targeted correction increases demand beyond passive modeling, but remains more supported than independent writing (comparable to mid-level guided correction). |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce target spelling pattern qu for /k/ + gesture practice (choral /k/) | Articulation compare/contrast /k/ vs /g/ with partner talk + hand-on-throat check | + | Moves from simple choral production/gesture (low) to analytic comparison and explanation (middle). |
| 2 | Articulation compare/contrast /k/ vs /g/ with partner talk + hand-on-throat check | Sound Switch-a-Roo with /k/ spelled c/k/qu (identify which spelling fits in spoken words) | + | Moves from phoneme feature analysis (middle) to high-level phoneme-grapheme decision/switching in words (Form D high). |
| 3 | Sound Switch-a-Roo with /k/ spelled c/k/qu (identify which spelling fits in spoken words) | Sound Puzzle to build words with qu + other phonemes (blend to make words) | + | Both are Form D high-level manipulation tasks; Sound Puzzle sustains high demand (blending/word construction) and is not simpler than Switch-a-Roo. |
| 4 | Sound Puzzle to build words with qu + other phonemes (blend to make words) | Teacher modeling of uppercase Q and lowercase q formation with verbal stroke cues (students watch, count strokes, answer what’s same/different) | - | This is a true drop from high phonological manipulation (Sound Puzzle) to low-demand teacher modeling/observation of letter formation. It does not fit the listed valid non-escalation patterns (it’s not “apply same skill in simpler response format,” not assessment→feedback, and not a brief end-of-section comprehension check). It’s a topic shift (phonology → handwriting) that reduces cognitive demand within the section. |

Feedback is also consistent with Pattern #2 (corrective feedback) following initial attempts/observations. |

**Application**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Teacher think-aloud model spelling “quick” with qu and ck (demonstration) | middle | A modeled encoding/analysis routine: teacher segments and maps to graphemes while students observe. Students are not yet producing full written responses, so it is less demanding than student-led encoding tasks but more complex than simple identification. |
| 2 | What’s that Pattern: segment/count sounds with chips/Elkonin boxes and write letters for each sound (focus on qu) | high | Direct match to Form D Application “What’s that Pattern” (high): segment sounds and write corresponding graphemes/patterns. |
| 3 | Sound Switch Writing: write a word then change one phoneme/grapheme to make a new word (keep qu constant) | high | Direct match to Form D “Sound Switch Writing” (high): write word then substitute one sound/letter to form new word. |
| 4 | Corrective feedback: contrast kit vs quit; students fix spellings to use qu | middle | Teacher-led error analysis and correction of a confusable spelling choice; lower demand than generating/switching words but more than simple recall. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Teacher think-aloud model spelling “quick” with qu and ck (demonstration) | What’s that Pattern: segment/count sounds with chips/Elkonin boxes and write letters for each sound (focus on qu) | + | Moves from supported modeling (middle) to student-applied segmentation+encoding (Form D high). |
| 2 | What’s that Pattern: segment/count sounds with chips/Elkonin boxes and write letters for each sound (focus on qu) | Sound Switch Writing: write a word then change one phoneme/grapheme to make a new word (keep qu constant) | + | Both are high-level. Sound Switch Writing typically adds an additional manipulation requirement (maintain part of the word, change one sound/letter), so it is at least as complex. |
| 3 | Sound Switch Writing: write a word then change one phoneme/grapheme to make a new word (keep qu constant) | Corrective feedback: contrast kit vs quit; students fix spellings to use qu | + | High → middle drop is acceptable under Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #2 (Corrective Feedback After Assessment/Performance): feedback responds to errors observed during the switching/writing task and immediately remediates the qu vs k choice. |

**Judge Summary:** Sections 1, 3, and 4 show generally sound within-section scaffolding: they start with supported routines and move into higher-demand discrimination/writing, with any difficulty drops explained by planned corrective feedback (a valid non-escalation pattern). Section 2 is mostly sequenced from simpler to more complex within the phonology/spelling portion, but it contains one clear within-section sequencing break: it moves from high-level phonological manipulation (Sound Puzzle) down to low-demand teacher-only letter-formation modeling. That drop is a topic shift (phonology to handwriting) rather than a practice-then-apply simplification or feedback cycle, so it is the main within-section scaffolding concern.

#### Across: 100.0% (8/8 links)

| # | Earlier (Section) | Later (Section) | Builds? | Reasoning |
|---|-------------------|-----------------|---------|-----------|
| 1 | Sort the sound (voiced vs unvoiced, including /k/ vs /g/) (Review & Connect) | Articulation/voicing comparison of /k/ vs /g/ and focus on /k/ as unvoiced; connect /k/ to spellings k, c, qu (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Section 1 warms up a key perceptual routine (monitoring voicing with a hand on the throat and categorizing sounds as voiced/unvoiced), explicitly rehearsing /k/ contrasted with /g/. Section 2 immediately reuses that same routine to sharpen discrimination of the target phoneme /k/ before mapping it to graphemes (k/c/qu). This is a legitimate easier-to-harder progression: auditory/kinesthetic feature sorting → applying that feature awareness to anchor the new spelling generalization for /k/. |
| 2 | Sort the sound (attend to phoneme features; respond on cue; choral production) (Review & Connect) | Sound Switch-a-Roo (decide how initial /k/ is spelled: c vs k vs qu) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | The review establishes attentional control and accurate production of /k/ (and contrasts) so students can reliably identify the initial phoneme in spoken words. Sound Switch-a-Roo is harder because students must (1) isolate the initial /k/ in real words and (2) select among multiple spellings. The review doesn’t teach the spelling choice, but it primes the prerequisite listening-and-responding routine and strengthens /k/ identification needed for the later spelling decision. |
| 3 | Sound Switch-a-Roo (identify initial /k/ and choose spelling c/k/qu) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound Puzzle (blend phonemes using /k/ written as qu to make words like quiz/quit/quip) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sound Switch-a-Roo builds the concept that /k/ can be represented by different graphemes and spotlights the special use of qu. Sound Puzzle then increases difficulty by requiring students to hold phonemes in sequence, blend them, and specifically treat qu as the written representation for the initial /k/ (and implicitly the /kw/ sequence in these examples). This is a clear conceptual escalation: selecting a spelling for a sound in a given word → constructing and blending words where the target spelling is embedded. |
| 4 | Teacher models uppercase Q and lowercase q with verbal stroke cues and start-point/line placement contrasts (Model, Explain & Engage) | Top to Bottom Challenge; Line Talkers; Cover & Try (students write independently with accuracy/placement/fluency focus) (Guided Practice) | + | Section 2 provides the demonstration and explicit formation language (stroke sequence + where to start on the lines). Section 3 appropriately shifts to student production with progressively greater independence and metacognitive checking: (a) Top to Bottom Challenge reinforces starting position and stroke order, (b) Line Talkers requires describing line placement (more advanced monitoring), and (c) Cover & Try removes the visual model, increasing retrieval demand. This is a strong model → supported practice → independent attempt progression. |
| 5 | Sound Puzzle and explicit statement that /k/ at word start can be spelled qu (e.g., quiz/quit/quip) (Model, Explain & Engage) | What’s that Pattern (segment/count sounds; write graphemes for each, emphasizing qu for initial /k/) (Application) | + | In Section 2, students already practice building and blending the same qu-words orally with the teacher controlling the grapheme choice (qu is given). Application is harder: students must segment the full word, count phonemes, and independently encode—deciding to write the qu pattern for the initial sound(s) before writing the remaining letters. The earlier word-building work meaningfully primes the mapping of the target spelling to the target sound within whole-word encoding. |
| 6 | Explicit instruction that q works with u (qu) to spell /k/ at the beginning; compare options c/k/qu (Model, Explain & Engage) | Teacher think-aloud spelling quick using qu and explaining why; then students spell quit/quip (Application) | + | Section 2 introduces the core spelling generalization (qu as a special /k/ spelling at the beginning) and contrasts it with other /k/ spellings. Section 4 leverages that knowledge in an authentic encoding context via a worked example (think-aloud) and then transfers responsibility to students. This is an appropriate conceptual scaffold: introduce and discriminate the new correspondence → apply it in word spelling with rationale. |
| 7 | Accurate formation of uppercase Q and lowercase q with line placement and independent retrieval (Cover & Try) (Guided Practice) | Write qu within words during What’s that Pattern and Sound Switch Writing (Application) | + | Section 3 strengthens motor plans and legibility for q/Q so that, in Section 4, handwriting load is reduced when students must encode words containing qu. While Application focuses more on spelling than isolated letter formation, the ability to write q efficiently and correctly supports successful, fluent word writing and prevents formation errors (e.g., q/p reversals) from interfering with spelling practice. |
| 8 | What’s that Pattern (segment and encode qu-words) (Application) | Sound Switch Writing (write quip/quit; change one phoneme while preserving qu) (Application) | + | Within Section 4, What’s that Pattern establishes full-word segmentation and initial encoding with qu. Sound Switch Writing then adds an additional layer: students must hold the base spelling constant, manipulate one sound/letter, and preserve the qu pattern—an increase in cognitive flexibility and orthographic attention. This is a valid step-up from initial encoding to controlled phoneme-grapheme manipulation. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson generally follows a logical scaffold: it begins with easier phonological feature awareness and /k/ discrimination (voicing sort), then moves to higher-level phoneme–grapheme decision-making for /k/ (c/k/qu) and word building with qu, then transitions to explicit teacher modeling of Q/q formation followed by increasingly independent handwriting practice, and finally applies both spelling knowledge (qu for /k/ at word start) and handwriting fluency to encode and manipulate real qu-words. The cross-section links are substantive and mostly prerequisite-based (sound discrimination → spelling choice; modeling → guided production; encoding → sound-switch manipulation).

#### Release: 71.4% (5/7 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2 — Model, Explain & Engage | high | "My turn" / explicit modeling throughout (e.g., "My turn first"; "Watch me closely. No one is writing yet"); Teacher provides exaggerated articulation and directs attention: "Look at my mouth and listen"; Choral responding with tight teacher cueing: "Ready…Go" routines for sounds and statements; Teacher performs full demonstrations of tasks (Sound Switch-a-Roo example with *cat*; Sound Puzzle example forming *quiz*); Letter formation is fully modeled with scripted stroke cues and line-placement: "I start at the top line…"; "Watch. I start at the middle line"; Immediate corrective modeling and contrast examples (p vs q; O vs Q) with explicit fix-it directions |
| Section 3 — Guided Practice | medium | Students produce the writing product repeatedly ("Write one uppercase Q"; "Write two more"; "Write one lowercase q"); Teacher continues to supply procedural scaffolds (stroke scripts: "Big curve around…" / "Rainbow around…"); Teacher provides strong starting/placement prompts: "Where do we start…?" and "Everyone put your pencil on the top line"; Ongoing monitoring and inline correction: "Teacher scans and adjusts grips"; "Teacher circulates"; fix missing stroke "add the little line out now"; Some peer verbalization (Line Talkers partner talk) but still within teacher-directed structure; Cover & Try introduces partial removal of the visual model ("cover the model… without looking") |
| Section 4 — Application | medium | Section begins with teacher think-aloud model: "Let me show you. My turn" for spelling *quick*; Students apply in words (quit/quip) with multi-step phoneme mapping plus writing; High structure remains: choral stretch/segment routines ("Stretch the sounds…"; "Tap and count") and reminders ("Remember… use qu"); Teacher still provides corrective feedback and directs fixes ("Everyone who wrote kit, fix it now"); However, students generate written words independently on their papers after prompts ("Your turn… write quit"; "Your turn… write quip"; "change quip to quit") |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2 — Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3 — Guided Practice | + | Support decreases because students shift from watching/listening and choral responses ("No one is writing yet") to producing multiple written Q/q forms themselves ("Write one… Write two more…"). Teacher still prompts and monitors, but productive responsibility moves to students. |
| 2 | Section 3 — Guided Practice | Section 4 — Application | + | Although Section 4 includes a new teacher model/think-aloud, the task demand increases substantially (single-letter formation → spelling/encoding words, segmentation, and sound switching). Teacher structure (think-aloud, segmentation prompts, reminders to use **qu**) is a justified cognitive-load trade-off while students apply skills to a more complex context by writing words (quit/quip) and making edits (switch writing). Overall responsibility shifts from letter formation practice to applying Q/q in authentic spelling. |

| 5 | Section 3 Step 5 — Cover & Try for lowercase q (trace model, then write without model) | Section 3 Step 6 — Independent repetitions for challenge (two more lowercase q’s) with teacher circulation | + | Students continue writing without reintroducing the model; teacher language shifts to brief success criteria and monitoring. This sustains/extends the independent phase with minimal added scaffolds. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 2→3→4, the lesson generally follows a gradual release arc. Section 2 is teacher-led modeling and explicit explanation (high support). Section 3 moves students into producing the letters with coaching and structured routines (medium), including an internal progression that culminates in Cover & Try and repeated independent writing (low). Section 4 increases overall task complexity (from forming a single letter to encoding whole words with **qu**, segmenting sounds, and switching phonemes). Teacher support remains moderate (think-aloud model, segmentation prompts, corrective feedback), but this is an appropriate cognitive-load trade-off because students are applying the taught grapheme pattern and handwriting in more complex, meaning-bearing tasks. Within Section 3, the clearest release happens at Cover & Try (model removed), and the final repetitions maintain low support. One midstream transition (Top to Bottom → Line Talkers) is a lateral move rather than a support decrease, but it does not break the overall scaffolding progression.

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 3–4 show strong logical scaffolding. The lesson explicitly teaches (Section 2) the critical new orthographic concept (qu for /k/ at the beginning, representing /k/+/w/) and explicitly models letter formation for Q/q before students are asked to write independently (Section 3). Guided Practice appropriately sequences motor rehearsal (sky-writing), supported production (Top to Bottom Challenge, Line Talkers), and memory-based production (Cover & Try) prior to spelling applications.

In Section 4, the two high-level spelling tasks (What’s that Pattern; Sound Switch Writing) have their core component skills supported: segmentation is practiced earlier through repeated “stretch” routines, and phoneme→grapheme mapping for qu is explicitly taught and rehearsed. Sound-counting with boxes/chips is introduced within the Application activity via clear modeling, which functions as an embedded scaffold rather than a missing prerequisite.

Minor note: the lesson assumes students can already write and encode the non-target letters (u, i, t, p, z, etc.), which is reasonable for 1st grade and consistent with the rubric’s ‘previously mastered skills count as taught’ guidance. No major missing component sub-skills were identified for the Section 3–4 complex tasks.

**Judge Summary:** Section 3 has a complete modeled motor-task demonstration immediately prior (in Section 2) and students are prompted to verbalize each stroke in order while writing, which functions as detailed modeling for handwriting. However, the Guided Practice portion does not explicitly tie the letter formation back to the sound (/k/ spelled with qu) during writing, so sound-to-letter connection is not made in that section. Section 4 clearly meets the criterion: it includes a full teacher think-aloud with a specific word (quick) that narrates internal decision-making (choosing qu vs. c/k, mapping /i/ to i, selecting ck) step-by-step and explicitly connects sounds to spellings (e.g., “/k/… write qu,” “/i/… letter i”).

**Judge Summary:** The lesson includes four clear corrective-feedback blocks across Sections 1–4. Each block (1) anticipates a specific, developmentally plausible error (phoneme confusion /g/ vs /k/; letter-formation confusions q vs p; omitting the Q tail; using k instead of qu), (2) provides scripted teacher dialogue a teacher could read, (3) explains the discriminating feature (voicing/buzzing; tail side; tail stroke; grapheme choice k vs qu), and (4) actively engages students through contrast examples, partner talk, chorally responding, and immediate self-correction/fixing work.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the plan frequently includes post-response explanations after the first and/or second elicited response, especially for voicing (buzz vs no buzz), articulation (/k/ vs /g/ tongue/vocal box), letter features (Q vs O; uppercase vs lowercase start points), and spelling logic (qu as the beginning pattern). A few instances are only confirmation or no acknowledgment (e.g., identifying the first sound /k/ in ‘kit’ gets no follow-up; some start-point questions get only ‘Yes’). Overall, the lesson meets the criterion because most key activities provide an explicit why/explanatory follow-up early in the response sequence.

**Judge Summary:** Yes—multiple compare/contrast moments appear across the lesson. The plan includes a strong phoneme contrast (/k/ vs /g/) with explicit similarity, difference, articulatory/voicing detail, and students producing both sounds. It also includes grapheme-level comparisons for spelling /k/ (k/c/qu) embedded in activities and corrective feedback. For letter formation, it explicitly contrasts lowercase q with p and uppercase Q with O using shared features and distinguishing strokes/placement; these include letter-form (stroke/position) detail and students at least revise/produce the target letter, though they are not always asked to write both letters.

Overall specificity is strong, with only light reliance on summarized directions.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, many question prompts have specific, checkable verbal responses (e.g., /g/, /k/, “qu”). The main gaps are (1) partner-talk responses labeled only as “(Partner talk)”—these are not specific enough to verify; and (2) many directives/practice tasks that require observable actions (hand on vocal box, sky-writing, placing pencil, tracing/writing while saying cues) either have no student response at all or include only a generic action (“Students write/trace”) with the verbal component missing. To meet the feedback goal, add parenthetical responses that explicitly state BOTH what students do and what they say for practice tasks, and add sample expected language for partner-talk prompts.

**Model, Explain & Engage**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce /r/ articulation & voicing (mirror/throat check, engine-hand gesture; choral /r/) | low | Closest to Form D Sound Spark/Tap that Sound style introduction: students primarily imitate/produce the target phoneme with teacher modeling and gestures; no segmentation/manipulation or writing required. |
| 2 | Compare/contrast /r/ vs /l/ (articulation discussion; produce /r/ then /l/) | middle | Not listed verbatim in Form D; best fit is a phoneme-discrimination/analysis task. Students must attend to articulatory features to distinguish confusable phonemes, which is more cognitively demanding than simple imitation, but less demanding than phoneme manipulation (e.g., Chop-a-Sound) or generating/spelling. |
| 3 | Sound Hunt (identify words with initial /r/ using gesture/card response) | low | Matches Form D Sound Hunt (low): listen to words and indicate when target sound is heard (here, initial position only) using a simple gesture response. |
| 4 | Connect /r/ to letter R/r + teacher models stroke sequence on handwriting lines (no student writing yet) | middle | Not a named Form D activity, but it is explicit letter-formation modeling with stroke cues and line placement. This requires linking phoneme to grapheme forms and attending to stroke sequence/starting points (upper vs lower), which is more complex than oral sound ID, but still scaffolded because students are only observing/responding orally. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce /r/ articulation & voicing (mirror/throat check, engine-hand gesture; choral /r/) | Compare/contrast /r/ vs /l/ (articulation discussion; produce /r/ then /l/) | + | Moves from simple imitation/production of /r/ (low) to analytic discrimination between two voiced liquids /r/ and /l/ (middle), increasing cognitive demand appropriately. |
| 2 | Compare/contrast /r/ vs /l/ (articulation discussion; produce /r/ then /l/) | Sound Hunt (identify words with initial /r/ using gesture/card response) | + | Although Sound Hunt is labeled low, this fits Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #1 (apply a harder-taught skill in a simpler response format). The comparison work builds the discrimination technique; Sound Hunt then has students apply that listening/discrimination to whole words using an easy gesture response. |
| 3 | Sound Hunt (identify words with initial /r/ using gesture/card response) | Connect /r/ to letter R/r + teacher models stroke sequence on handwriting lines (no student writing yet) | + | Progresses from auditory identification of /r/ in words (low) to grapheme connection plus observing/encoding stroke sequence and line placement (middle). This is a logical increase toward handwriting production. |

**Guided Practice**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Magic Finger Write (uppercase R; finger tracing with verbal stroke cues) | low | Matches Form D Magic Finger Write (low): trace letter shapes with finger while following modeling/verbal cues; lower fine-motor and memory demand than pencil writing. |
| 2 | Magic Finger Write (lowercase r; finger tracing with verbal stroke cues) | low | Same Form D activity as above; still finger-based rehearsal (low). Variation in starting line (middle) does not change the activity’s overall demand level. |
| 3 | Trace & Say (uppercase R on dotted letters with pencil) | middle | Matches Form D Trace & Say (middle): pencil tracing on dotted templates while saying strokes; adds tool control and visual-motor precision compared to finger tracing. |
| 4 | Corrective Feedback #1 (uppercase R start point/stroke-order error correction; contrast incorrect vs correct; then retrace) | middle | This is targeted error analysis and reteaching around stroke order/starting position. It aligns with the kind of metacognitive ‘checking’ demands seen in higher handwriting tasks, but here students are primarily analyzing models and then re-tracing, so overall it functions as a mid-level remediation step. |
| 5 | Trace & Say (lowercase r on dotted letters with pencil) | middle | Same Form D Trace & Say (middle) applied to lowercase r: pencil tracing with stroke talk. |
| 6 | Corrective Feedback #2 (r vs n confusion; analyze difference; circle error; then write a corrected small r next to it) | high | Goes beyond tracing: students must self-monitor, identify an incorrect formation (r resembling n), and then produce a corrected r adjacent to it (more like a brief independent write/‘cover & try’ moment). This adds decision-making + production without a full trace model, increasing cognitive and motor demand above typical Trace & Say. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Magic Finger Write (uppercase R; finger tracing with verbal stroke cues) | Magic Finger Write (lowercase r; finger tracing with verbal stroke cues) | + | Same low-level activity format; maintains demand while shifting to the second letterform (uppercase to lowercase), which is a reasonable progression before introducing pencils. |
| 2 | Magic Finger Write (lowercase r; finger tracing with verbal stroke cues) | Trace & Say (uppercase R on dotted letters with pencil) | + | Low to middle progression: finger tracing rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) precedes pencil-based dotted tracing (Trace & Say), consistent with Form D scaffolding. |
| 3 | Trace & Say (uppercase R on dotted letters with pencil) | Corrective Feedback #1 (uppercase R start point/stroke-order error correction; contrast incorrect vs correct; then retrace) | + | Difficulty does not inappropriately drop; this matches Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #2 (corrective feedback after an assessment/practice attempt). The feedback directly responds to observed errors and prepares students to continue correct practice. |
| 4 | Corrective Feedback #1 (uppercase R start point/stroke-order error correction; contrast incorrect vs correct; then retrace) | Trace & Say (lowercase r on dotted letters with pencil) | + | Returns to the core middle-level practice format (Trace & Say) for the next letterform. Even if the feedback moment felt slightly more analytical, this is a standard practice block shift (finish uppercase support, then practice lowercase) rather than a true regression. |
| 5 | Trace & Say (lowercase r on dotted letters with pencil) | Corrective Feedback #2 (r vs n confusion; analyze difference; circle error; then write a corrected small r next to it) | + | Moves from middle (guided dotted tracing) to a more demanding self-monitoring + correction + independent production step (high). This is an appropriate increase at the end of guided practice. |

**Application**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Teacher think-aloud: segment CVC word (ran) with sound boxes; model writing letters for each phoneme | middle | Not a direct Form D label in Application; it resembles modeling for Mystery Word Builder/Sound-letter mapping with segmentation. Because it is teacher-modeled (students observe), it’s less demanding than students doing the full task, but it still involves explicit phoneme segmentation and grapheme selection, so best placed at middle. |
| 2 | Write the Sound You Hear (students write the first sound/letter for spoken words) | low | Matches Form D Write the Sound You Hear (low): teacher says a sound/word and students write the matching letter; here constrained to initial phoneme only, which is consistent with the low-level application format. |
| 3 | Write the Sound You Hear (students write the last sound/letter /r/ for spoken words) | low | Same Form D activity (low). Listening for final position is slightly harder than initial for many students, but the response remains single-letter writing in a highly structured routine, so it still fits low overall. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Teacher think-aloud: segment CVC word (ran) with sound boxes; model writing letters for each phoneme | Write the Sound You Hear (students write the first sound/letter for spoken words) | + | Apparent difficulty drop (middle to low) is acceptable under Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #1: the think-aloud teaches the technique (listen/segment, decide which sound position matters, map to letter), then students apply it in a simpler response format (write just one letter for the target position). |
| 2 | Write the Sound You Hear (students write the first sound/letter for spoken words) | Write the Sound You Hear (students write the last sound/letter /r/ for spoken words) | + | Same activity type and response mode (low) with a mild increase in listening challenge (final position), so the sequence is at least equal in difficulty and arguably slightly harder. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the within-section sequencing is generally scaffolded from simpler oral/gestural tasks to more complex discrimination, grapheme/handwriting modeling, then supported motor practice (finger → pencil tracing) and finally application writing. The only intentional ‘dip’ is in Section 2 (comparison to Sound Hunt) and Section 4 (teacher segmentation model to simpler student task), both of which fit the valid pattern of teaching a higher-level skill/strategy and then practicing it with a simpler response format. Guided Practice appropriately escalates from Magic Finger Write (low) to Trace & Say (middle) and ends with higher-demand self-monitoring/correction.

| 5 | Magic Finger Write (air/desk/paper tracing with strokes aloud) (Guided Practice) | Trace & Say (pencil tracing dotted R and r with strokes aloud) (Guided Practice) | + | Finger tracing builds gross-to-fine motor planning and reinforces the stroke sequence and starting point without pencil-control demands. Trace & Say then increases difficulty by adding tool use (pencil grip), finer motor control, and tighter line adherence while keeping the same verbal cues—an appropriate progression. |
| 6 | Compare /r/ vs /l/ (articulatory discrimination) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Corrective feedback to prevent formation confusions (e.g., r vs n; start-point errors for R) (Guided Practice) | + | Although the confusions differ (sound-level /r/ vs /l/; letter-level r vs n), Section 2 establishes the lesson norm of explicitly contrasting ‘look/feel’ differences to avoid near-neighbor confusion. That analytic compare/contrast routine is then applied to handwriting in Guided Practice when students are shown side-by-side examples and named differences. |
| 7 | Sound–letter link stated explicitly (“/r/ is written with R/r”) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Write the Sound You Hear (write the letter for initial /r/ and final /r/) (Application) | + | Application requires students to select the grapheme based on hearing the phoneme in a word. The explicit mapping taught in Section 2 is the prerequisite for this task; without it, students could hear /r/ but not know what to write. |
| 8 | Trace & Say formation practice for uppercase R and lowercase r with line placement and stroke order (Guided Practice) | Write the Sound You Hear (students write r when they hear /r/) (Application) | + | Application adds the cognitive load of phoneme identification within words plus retrieval of the correct letter, so automaticity with letter formation matters. Trace & Say supports forming r correctly (start at middle line, tiny curve) so that when students must write quickly in response to sounds, the motor plan is available and less likely to collapse into common confusions (e.g., r→n). |
| 9 | Sound Hunt focuses on initial /r/ discrimination (Model, Explain & Engage) | Write the Sound You Hear includes both initial /r/ and final /r/ responses (Application) | - | The move from identifying /r/ only in the initial position to writing r for final-position /r/ is an increase in difficulty/complexity, but the lesson doesn’t provide earlier, dedicated oral practice for hearing /r/ in final position (or position-shifting) before asking students to encode it. A brief ‘Sound Hunt—end sound’ set or a quick Hide-and-Seek Sound (beginning vs end) would more directly prime the final-/r/ application. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 1–4 show strong scaffolding from phoneme response routines (Section 1) → explicit target sound discrimination and sound–letter mapping plus modeled strokes (Section 2) → supported motor practice from gross-to-fine (Section 3) → integrated sound-to-letter writing in a simple application task (Section 4). The main across-section gap is that Application asks for final-/r/ encoding without earlier section practice explicitly targeting final-position /r/ listening; everything else builds in a logical, increasing-difficulty sequence.

#### Release: 80.0% (4/5 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | high | "My turn. Listen to my sound: /rrr/"; "Watch my mouth" + explicit articulatory placement (tongue/lips) and voicing check (hand on throat); Choral prompting throughout: "Everyone... Get ready… Go."; Teacher-led discrimination routine: compares /r/ vs /l/ with teacher demonstrations of both; "Sound Hunt" is tightly scripted with teacher modeling: "My turn first... I hear /r/..." then students respond yes/no via gesture; Handwriting is fully modeled on the board with stroke-by-stroke verbal cues: "Watch me closely... Watch my hand as I say the strokes" + children only chant while watching |
| Section 3: Guided Practice | medium | Begins with teacher model then student attempt: "My turn first... Watch" → "Now your turn... Go"; Verbal procedural scaffold remains constant: students "write and say" the full stroke script (e.g., "Big line down, rainbow bump, slant out"); Physical/visual scaffolds are provided in Trace & Say: "dotted letters" and "start dot"; Teacher monitoring and correction is explicit: "Teacher circulates and checks"; "Teacher quickly corrects grips"; Inline reteaching/corrective feedback moments: wrong start point/stroke order; r vs n confusion; teacher shows incorrect vs correct models |
| Section 4: Application | medium | Teacher still models one example with a think-aloud: "Let me show you first. My turn. The word is ran."; Task is student-executed responses on teacher prompts: "Now it’s your turn... Write the letter that spells that sound."; Reduced handwriting scaffold relative to Section 3: children write letters without dotted letter templates ("first blank" / "next blank" on an application sheet/line); Still largely choral/paced by teacher: repeated "Think… Get ready… Go" and immediate feedback/confirmation after each item |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3: Guided Practice | + | Productive responsibility shifts from teacher-only modeling (students watch/say) to students physically producing letter forms (finger writing then pencil tracing). Although Section 3 adds some supports (dotted letters/start dots, teacher monitoring), this is appropriate because it introduces new motor demands (writing on lines, then pencil control). Overall, responsibility increases for students, so support is considered decreased via a justified cognitive-load trade-off. |
| 2 | Section 3: Guided Practice | Section 4: Application | + | The task shifts from forming a single letter with formation scaffolds (dotted/start-dot tracing + full stroke scripts) to applying sound-to-letter mapping in spoken words (beginning and final phoneme focus) while writing the matching letter. Even though the teacher still structures the routine with call-and-response and models one example, students must independently decide which letter to write based on phonemic analysis, and they write without tracing templates. Increased cognitive demand (phoneme position listening) is balanced by keeping the writing demand to single letters, making this a valid release. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | 3.1 Magic Finger Write – Uppercase R | high | "My turn first in the air. Watch."; Teacher gives exact placement cue: "Put your magic finger at the top line"; Choral pacing: "Get ready… Write and say. Go."; Teacher monitoring: "Teacher circulates and checks"; Students must say full stroke script while moving: "Big line down, rainbow bump, slant out" |
| 2 | 3.2 Magic Finger Write – Lowercase r | high | "Put your magic finger on the middle line"; "My turn: Little line down, tiny curve out"; "Your turn... Write and say" with full verbal cue |
| 3 | 3.3 Trace & Say – Uppercase R (includes brief independent traces + CF #1) | medium | Introduces pencil production (new motor demand) while providing strong physical scaffolds: "dotted letters" + "start dot"; Choral guided attempts: "We will trace and say the strokes together"; Some independence is added: "then 1–2 independently while teacher monitors"; Corrective feedback re-teaches error patterns with side-by-side models (incorrect vs correct R) |
| 4 | 3.4 Trace & Say – Lowercase r (includes independent traces + CF #2 with write-next-to/circle) | medium | Still uses dotted letters and start dots: "lowercase r dotted letters" + "middle line start dot"; Mix of guided and independent: "trace 3–4... together" then "trace 2–3 more independently while you circulate"; Error analysis scaffold: teacher contrasts n vs r on board; students chant a rule; students self-identify an error: "Circle it" then "write a small r right next to it" |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | 3.1 Magic Finger Write – Uppercase R | 3.2 Magic Finger Write – Lowercase r | - | Support level stays high: both are teacher-modeled, choral, finger-based rehearsals with explicit start-line placement and full stroke scripts. The letter changes, but the scaffolding does not meaningfully fade. |
| 2 | 3.2 Magic Finger Write – Lowercase r | 3.3 Trace & Say – Uppercase R (includes brief independent traces + CF #1) | + | Even though dotted letters/start dots add physical scaffolding, the step introduces a new fine-motor demand (pencil control) and shifts students to producing written marks on paper. The dotted template appropriately offsets the new motor load; overall productive responsibility increases, so this is a valid release. |
| 3 | 3.3 Trace & Say – Uppercase R (includes brief independent traces + CF #1) | 3.4 Trace & Say – Lowercase r (includes independent traces + CF #2 with write-next-to/circle) | + | Support is similar (still tracing), but 3.4 includes an added independence move: students must locate an error in their own work ("Circle it") and then write a corrected r next to it—more self-monitoring than in 3.3. Task difficulty (avoiding n) increases, yet the teacher keeps dotted/start-dot supports, so overall responsibility is slightly higher and the transition counts as a modest release. |

**Judge Summary:** The lesson largely follows gradual release from Section 2 to 4. Section 2 is teacher-owned modeling and sound discrimination with heavy choral control. Section 3 moves students into production via a sound motor progression (finger write → pencil Trace & Say), appropriately balancing increased motor demands with dotted/start-dot scaffolds and corrective feedback. Section 4 increases application complexity (phoneme position listening + grapheme selection in words) while reducing formation scaffolds (no tracing templates), keeping the writing demand to single letters—an appropriate cognitive-load trade-off. Within Section 3, the key release occurs from Magic Finger Write to Trace & Say; support does not fade all the way to independent blank-line writing, but for '1st grade, struggling' this medium-support endpoint is developmentally reasonable.

Section 2 explicitly models both letters on handwriting lines and has students say the cues while watching. |
| Trace & Say (uppercase R and lowercase r on dotted templates; some independent tries) (Guided Practice (Section 3)) | Understand line placement and starting points on handwriting lines (uppercase starts at top line; lowercase starts at middle line) | + | Teacher handwriting modeling + explicit compare/contrast of start points (Model, Explain & Engage (Section 2)) | To trace accurately, students must start at the correct line and keep the letter within the appropriate bounds. Section 2 directly teaches where each letter starts and checks understanding ("What is different about where I start…?"). |

Magic Finger Write immediately precedes tracing and provides that rehearsal with the same stroke cues. |

The lesson explicitly has students show pencil grip and then traces multiple letters together with teacher support; this is sufficient scaffolding within Section 3 for the tracing demand. |
| Trace & Say (uppercase R and lowercase r on dotted templates; some independent tries) (Guided Practice (Section 3)) | Discriminate lowercase r from a confusable formation (n) and adjust size/curve accordingly | + | Corrective Feedback #2 (r vs n comparison; rewrite small r) (Guided Practice (Section 3)) | Accurate completion of Trace & Say (and the later application writing) depends on not over-curving into an n-shape. The lesson explicitly teaches the visual/motor difference and provides immediate corrective practice. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Identify whether /r/ is present in the specified word position (beginning or end) | + | Sound Hunt (beginning /r/) + explicit instruction about hearing /r/ (Model, Explain & Engage (Section 2)) | Application asks students to listen for /r/ at the beginning (rug) and at the end (car/star/jar). Section 2 provides structured listening for initial /r/ via Sound Hunt and explicitly focuses attention on the target phoneme. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Ability to produce/discriminate the /r/ phoneme (including contrast with /l/) | + | /r/ articulation modeling + /r/ vs /l/ comparison (Model, Explain & Engage (Section 2)) | To map sound-to-letter, students must perceive/produce the sound reliably. The lesson explicitly teaches mouth/tongue placement, voicing, and contrasts /r/ with a confusable sound /l/. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Map phoneme /r/ to grapheme r (know that letter r spells /r/) | + | Explicit sound-to-letter connection ("The letter that matches /r/ is R") (Model, Explain & Engage (Section 2)) | Section 4 requires recalling which letter corresponds to /r/. Section 2 directly states the correspondence and links it to the Sound Hunt outcome ("When we hear /r/… we will write r"). |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Form lowercase r legibly when writing (letter formation, stroke order, and line placement) | + | Magic Finger Write + Trace & Say for lowercase r (with corrective feedback) (Guided Practice (Section 3)) | Section 4 has students write letters (primarily r). Section 3 gives multi-step practice moving from air/finger rehearsal to pencil tracing with stroke-language and correction, supporting independent single-letter writing. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Form any previously taught non-target letters that are required by the task (e.g., write m for the first sound in map) | + | Sound Spark (includes m and other known letters) (Review & Connect (Section 1)) | Students must write m for the word 'map' in Section 4. While Section 1 reviews the sound for m (not formation), the prompt notes these are previously learned letters; thus formation is assumed mastered and the review supports access to the sound-letter mapping. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | Phoneme segmentation into multiple sounds and selecting the correct target sound position as directed (first vs last) | + | Teacher Think-Aloud modeling stretching /rrr/-/a/-/n/ and identifying first/middle/last sounds (Application (Section 4)) | Even though the student task is 'write the first/last sound,' it still requires orienting to sound position. The teacher explicitly models stretching the word and labeling first/middle/last prior to student responses, providing immediate scaffolding before independent turns. |
| Write the Sound You Hear (Application: hear target position sound → write the matching letter; includes non-target letters like m) (Application (Section 4)) | For final-/r/ examples, distinguish phoneme /r/ from the r-controlled vowel chunk /ar/ in speech (hearing /r/ at the end) | - | — | The lesson uses examples like car/star/jar and models them as /c/–/ar/ and /s/–/t/–/ar/, which treat 'ar' as a chunk while still asking students to write the last sound /r/. For many struggling 1st graders, hearing /r/ as a separable final phoneme in r-controlled syllables is non-trivial and typically needs explicit instruction/practice (e.g., contrasting 'car' vs 'cat,' or isolating the final /r/ sound from /ar/ with clearer segmentation routines). No earlier section explicitly teaches how to isolate /r/ in r-controlled vowel endings; Section 2 Sound Hunt only targets initial /r/. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, the lesson shows strong within-lesson scaffolding for handwriting: Section 2 explicitly models stroke sequences and line placement, then Section 3 rehearses with Magic Finger Write and supported Trace & Say plus corrective feedback for common confusions (starting point errors; r vs n). The main decomposition gap appears in Application for final /r/ using r-controlled vowel examples (car/star/jar). Students are asked to isolate and write the 'last sound' as /r/ while the teacher models the spoken segmentation as /-ar/ chunks; there is no earlier explicit teaching/practice focused on isolating final /r/ in r-controlled endings. If the intent is simply 'write r when you hear /ar/,' the task language should match that; otherwise an explicit component lesson on hearing final /r/ in r-controlled syllables is missing.

#### SoundComp: 100.0% (4/4 items)

| Location | Target | Compared With | Similarity? | Difference? | Articulatory? | Students Produce? | Evidence |

| Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage – “Compare /r/ with a confusable sound /l/” | /r/ | /l/ | + | + | + | + | "“Now we are going to compare /r/ with another sound, /l/. Remember that we said that the /r/ sound is a lot like the /l/" |

**Judge Summary:** Yes—this lesson includes multiple explicit compare/contrast opportunities aligned to Criteria #6 and #11. It compares the target phoneme /r/ to a confusable phoneme /l/ with clear similarity/difference statements, articulatory detail (tongue placement/voicing), and student production of both sounds. It also compares letter forms (uppercase R vs lowercase r) and provides corrective feedback comparing lowercase r with confusable lowercase n, including how they are the same/different in stroke features and having students write the corrected form.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson is largely teacher-ready: it contains many verbatim teacher lines, clear call-and-response routines (e.g., 'Get ready… Go,' 'My turn/Your turn'), explicit articulatory explanations and a memory gesture for /r/, plus sequenced stroke cues for uppercase R and lowercase r. Stage directions are present (pointing, circulating, repeating sets), but they mostly support management and do not replace core instructional language. The main improvisation burden appears in Guided Practice where several braces lines summarize repetition/monitoring rather than scripting exact teacher prompts during independent attempts; however, the essential verbal cues for sound production and letter formation are explicitly provided.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, most choral sound responses and comparison questions include specific verbal child responses (e.g., /m/, /s/, /r/, 'top line'). The biggest gaps are in Guided Practice and Application: many directives have no written child action response (magic finger up, place finger/pencil, show grip, circle an error, finger under blank). Many practice tasks list only the verbal stroke cues but omit the required action (finger-write/trace/write). In Application, responses capture the writing action (write r/m) but omit the expected verbal identification of the sound (/r/, /m/) that the teacher explicitly asked for ('What is the first/last sound?'). Several responses are also too generic (e.g., 'students trace another R correctly') without specifying the stroke words and/or the exact action.

It supports accuracy within the same low-level oral activity. |

| 2 | Compare /c/ with /g/ (quiet vs buzzy; discriminate with hand on throat) | middle | Adds phoneme discrimination between confusable sounds using voicing contrast; this is more cognitively demanding than simple repetition because children must attend to a feature difference and categorize responses. |
| 3 | Sound Spark including new sound /c/ (mixed with known sounds) | low | Still Form D Sound Spark (low): teacher points to cards and students say sounds; inclusion of a new card doesn’t change the response mode—rapid oral retrieval only. |

| 5 | Teacher models letter formation for uppercase C and lowercase c with verbal cues; students chant cues while watching | low | This is explicit modeling and oral rehearsal of stroke language without independent writing; closest to low-level formation rehearsal (pre-pencil) akin to Sky Writing/Magic Finger Write preparation but even simpler because students mostly watch/chant. |
| 6 | Brief concept questions: what’s different/same about uppercase vs lowercase start points | middle | Requires recalling and verbally comparing two modeled forms (start at top vs middle); more cognitively complex than simple choral repetition because it involves analysis/comparison. |
| 7 | Corrective feedback: prevent closing c into o (visual contrast; identify open form) | middle | Error-correction contrasting confusable letterforms (c vs o) requires visual discrimination and feature identification (open vs closed), beyond basic modeling. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce /c/ articulation & voicing (hand on throat; gesture; choral practice) | Compare /c/ with /g/ (quiet vs buzzy; discriminate with hand on throat) | + | Moves from simple production/imitate to discrimination between two similar phonemes (added analysis), so difficulty appropriately increases (low → middle). |
| 2 | Compare /c/ with /g/ (quiet vs buzzy; discriminate with hand on throat) | Sound Spark including new sound /c/ (mixed with known sounds) | + | Although Form D labels Sound Spark as low, this is a valid non-escalation: students apply the just-taught discrimination/feature awareness in a simpler, rapid practice format (Valid Non-Escalation Pattern 1: skill application in a simpler response mode). |
| 3 | Sound Spark including new sound /c/ (mixed with known sounds) | Sound Hunt for initial /c/ (identify if word begins with /c/) | + | Both are low-level per Form D; Sound Hunt shifts from isolated phoneme response to identifying the sound in words, which is at least equal in cognitive demand even if both are labeled low. |
| 4 | Sound Hunt for initial /c/ (identify if word begins with /c/) | Teacher models letter formation for uppercase C and lowercase c with verbal cues; students chant cues while watching | + | This is a domain shift (phonemic listening → formation modeling). The formation step is still low-level (watch/chant, no writing), so it does not create an inappropriate difficulty drop or jump; it keeps demand controlled for struggling students. |
| 5 | Teacher models letter formation for uppercase C and lowercase c with verbal cues; students chant cues while watching | Brief concept questions: what’s different/same about uppercase vs lowercase start points | + | Asking children to compare/recall differences and similarities increases cognitive demand beyond choral cueing (low → middle). |

| 2 | Magic Finger Write (air-write uppercase C then lowercase c with cues) | Move to paper: Trace & Say routine on dotted uppercase C models | + | Appropriate escalation from finger rehearsal to pencil tracing with visual guides (low → middle) consistent with Form D progression. |

**Application**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Think-aloud modeling: segment CVC words and write beginning sound c in first box | middle | Although teacher is modeling (students mostly observe), the task demonstrated is akin to Application-level phoneme-to-grapheme encoding in a word frame (similar to Form D Mystery Word Builder / Sound-Letter Hunt elements). Because it’s modeled and only first-sound encoding, it sits around middle in underlying cognitive demand. |
| 2 | Trace the Sound You Hear (trace matching letter for spoken sound) | low | Matches Form D Trace the Sound You Hear (low): students trace the corresponding letter pattern when they hear the sound; dotted support reduces motor/cognitive load. |
| 3 | Write the Sound You Hear (write the letter for the beginning sound) | low | Matches Form D Write the Sound You Hear (low): students write the matching letter when they hear the sound; this is less supported than tracing but Form D still categorizes it as low in Application. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Think-aloud modeling: segment CVC words and write beginning sound c in first box | Trace the Sound You Hear (trace matching letter for spoken sound) | + | This is intentional scaffolding: teacher models a more complex skill routine (segment → identify first sound → encode) and then students practice the sound-letter match in a simpler response format (tracing) (Valid Non-Escalation Pattern 1). |
| 2 | Trace the Sound You Hear (trace matching letter for spoken sound) | Write the Sound You Hear (write the letter for the beginning sound) | + | Both are low per Form D, but writing without dotted tracing is typically equal or slightly harder than tracing, so the sequence is appropriate (low → low, increased independence). |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, the lesson’s within-section sequencing mostly follows a simple-to-complex progression appropriate for struggling kindergarteners: oral sound production/discrimination comes before word-level listening, which comes before letter-formation rehearsal without pencil, then pencil tracing, and finally more independent writing. A few places show an apparent drop in Form D difficulty labels (e.g., /c/ vs /g/ comparison to Sound Spark; modeling segmentation to tracing), but these fit valid scaffolding patterns: teach/contrast a skill, then practice it in a simpler response mode, and insert corrective feedback immediately after errors.

That exact response routine is then extended in Section 2 when the new c card is added; students are prepared to treat c as a sound-response task, not a naming task. |
| 2 | Sound Spark (rapid retrieval/fluency with sound cards) (Review & Connect) | /c/ vs /g/ articulation/voicing contrast + hand-on-throat check (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Although Section 1 does not preview /c/, it warms up general phonological attention and the expectation that speech sounds are the focus. That makes it easier to shift into careful listening/feeling (quiet vs buzzy) in the /c/ vs /g/ discrimination work in Section 2. |
| 3 | /c/ production and /c/ vs /g/ discrimination (quiet vs buzzy; gesture; hand-on-throat) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound Hunt (identify /c/ at the beginning of spoken words; cat/cup/can vs non-/c/) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Students first secure the target phoneme category (/c/ as unvoiced/quiet, not /g/) before being asked to detect it in words. That sequencing reduces confusion for struggling students when deciding whether a word begins with /c/. |
| 4 | Sound Hunt with teacher-led stretching/modeling (/c/-/a/-/t/; identify first sound) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Write the Sound You Hear (in words: stretch, identify first sound, write c) (Application) | + | Sound Hunt explicitly practices isolating the initial phoneme in CVC words after oral stretching—an essential prerequisite for encoding the beginning sound. Application then adds the harder step: not just identifying /c/ first, but writing the grapheme c to represent it. |
| 5 | Teacher models uppercase C and lowercase c formation with verbal cues; contrasts start points; keep c open (not o) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Finger Run + Magic Finger Write + supported tracing/writing of C/c on handwriting lines (Guided Practice) | + | Section 2 provides the explicit formation model (start location, stroke path, openness) and language cues. Guided Practice appropriately moves from orientation to lines (Finger Run) to gross-motor rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) to pencil-on-paper tracing/writing, directly practicing the modeled strokes with increasing motor demand. |
| 6 | Finger Run (top/middle/bottom lines; where to start; size) (Guided Practice) | Write the Sound You Hear (write lowercase c with correct starting point on lines) (Application) | + | Finger Run builds spatial/line awareness needed to execute the specific requirement in Application: writing lowercase c starting at the middle line (and not drifting to uppercase C formation). This is a genuine motor/placement prerequisite for accurate written responses during spelling. |
| 7 | Magic Finger Write + trace/write practice for lowercase c with cue 'Start at the middle, curve around' and feedback about not closing to o (Guided Practice) | Trace the Sound You Hear (trace c when /c/ is said) and Write the Sound You Hear (write c for initial /c/) (Application) | + | Application requires quick, repeatable production of the c shape (tracing, then independent writing) while attending to correct formation (start at middle, keep it open). Guided Practice establishes the motor plan and common error correction (c vs o; lowercase vs uppercase), so students can focus on sound-to-letter matching rather than inventing the letterform. |
| 8 | Sound Spark including c card (grapheme→phoneme for c) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Trace the Sound You Hear / Write the Sound You Hear (phoneme→grapheme: hear /c/ and select/produce c) (Application) | + | The lesson appropriately flips direction across sections: Section 2 practices recognizing the letter c and saying /c/ (decoding-style mapping), then Application requires the reverse (encoding-style mapping): hearing /c/ and producing c. Practicing both directions supports automaticity for struggling learners. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson mostly follows a logical scaffold for struggling kindergarteners: (1) warm up sound-response routines with known cards; (2) explicitly teach the new phoneme /c/ with a contrast to /g/, then practice detecting it in initial position and connect it to letter formation; (3) build handwriting readiness from line orientation to air-writing to supported pencil practice; (4) apply by moving from lower-demand tracing to higher-demand writing while also requiring phoneme isolation and sound-to-letter encoding. Overall, later tasks genuinely build on earlier component skills (phoneme discrimination → phoneme detection in words → letter formation → phoneme-to-grapheme spelling).

| Section 2 — Model, Explain & Engage | high | Frequent teacher-first framing: “My turn. Listen to my sound: /c/.”; “Watch my mouth.”; “Watch me closely. My turn. No one is writing yet.”; Choral/controlled response structure: “Your turn. Get ready… Go.”; “Say it with me…”; Teacher provides explicit articulatory instructions and checks: “Put your hand gently on your throat… Did your throat feel quiet or buzzy?”; Teacher fully models letter formation with a poster/board model and provides the complete stroke script: “Start at the top, curve around like a rainbow… Start at the middle, curve around…”; High teacher control of task rules in Sound Hunt: “If you hear /c/… put your hand on your head… If you do not… hands in lap.”; Corrective feedback is teacher-directed with immediate discrimination prompt: “Which one is open… Point with your finger. Ready… Go.” |
| Section 3 — Guided Practice | medium | Students begin producing the motor pattern (air-writing then pencil marks), but with continuous teacher cueing: “Say it with me… Ready… Go.”; Teacher provides strong start-point and co-action language: “Put your pencil where the top line begins… I will write with you.”; Physical scaffolds are present: “trace provided model C… next dotted C” (dotted templates/models); Inline correction and reteach are embedded: “Some of our lowercase c’s are starting… Watch… Erase and try again.”; Teacher circulates with direct support: “Teacher circulates and gives support, touching starting point.” |
| Section 4 — Application | medium | Application begins with teacher think-aloud modeling: “Let me show you. My turn… stretch the sounds… I will write c in the first box.”; First application task remains heavily scaffolded: “Trace the Sound You Hear” uses dotted letters and teacher calls the sounds: “Sound: /c/. Get ready… Go.”; Second application task reduces physical scaffolds: “You will not have dotted lines for every letter this time… listen, then write the letter…”; Teacher continues to provide procedural reminders during writing: “Remember… Start at the middle, curve around…”; Teacher monitoring continues: “Teacher circulates, checking they start at middle line and leave it open.” |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2 — Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3 — Guided Practice | + | Section 2 is primarily teacher demonstration and oral responding (“No one is writing yet… just watching”). In Section 3, students take on productive responsibility for forming C/c (air-writing then tracing), even though teacher cues remain. The shift from observe/model to guided production represents a clear release. |
| 2 | Section 3 — Guided Practice | Section 4 — Application | + | Section 4 raises task complexity from single-letter formation to using /c/ to encode the beginning sound in spoken words (“stretch the sounds… first sound… write c”). Teacher support remains moderate (think-aloud, prompts), and the first activity even uses tracing, which could look like an increase in physical scaffold. However, this is a justified cognitive-load trade-off: spelling-from-sound is harder than copying/tracing a single letter, and students are applying the letter-sound/formation skill in a new, integrated context. Additionally, within Section 4, scaffolds reduce (tracing → writing), reinforcing the release overall. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1 — Finger Run (line awareness warm-up) | high | Teacher directs each micro-movement: “put your finger on the top line… Slide… Say: middle line… Slide… bottom line.”; Choral call-and-response: “Say: top line… (Children: top line)”; No writing product; rehearsal only with teacher pacing: “Ready… Go.” |
| 2 | Section 3 Step 2 — Magic Finger Write (air-write uppercase C and lowercase c with cues) | high | No tool yet; teacher leads stroke-by-stroke with full verbal script: “Say it with me… Start at the top, curve around…” and “Start at the middle…”; Teacher-first then students: “Again. My turn in the air… Your turn.”; Repeated choral practice: multiple “Ready… Go” repetitions |
| 3 | Section 3 Step 3 — Pencil on paper: Trace & Say routine (dotted models) for uppercase then lowercase | medium | Students create a permanent written product (trace/write) rather than air-writing: “We are going to write on our paper… (Children trace provided model C)”; Physical scaffolds remain strong: “next dotted C” and “trace provided model C”; Teacher co-action and start-point cueing: “Put your pencil where the top line begins… I will write with you.”; Ongoing corrective feedback and re-dos: “Erase and try again… do lowercase c together.”; Teacher monitoring/touch prompts: “circulates… touching starting point.” |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1 — Finger Run (line awareness warm-up) | Section 3 Step 2 — Magic Finger Write (air-write uppercase C and lowercase c with cues) | + | Both steps are highly teacher-led, but Magic Finger Write increases student productive responsibility for the actual letter shape (not just identifying lines). Even though support remains high, responsibility shifts from line naming/moving to executing the letter-form motion, representing a small release. |
| 2 | Section 3 Step 2 — Magic Finger Write (air-write uppercase C and lowercase c with cues) | Section 3 Step 3 — Pencil on paper: Trace & Say routine (dotted models) for uppercase then lowercase | + | This transition introduces a new fine-motor demand (pencil control and writing on paper). The lesson adds/keeps physical scaffolds (dotted letters, explicit start-point cues) to offset that demand, but overall productive responsibility increases because students now produce permanent marks on the page. That counts as a release via cognitive-load trade-off. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 2→3→4 show an appropriate gradual release for struggling kindergarteners. Section 2 is clear I-do (teacher models sound production/discrimination and letter formation). Section 3 shifts to guided production with a sound motor progression (Finger Run → Magic Finger Write → pencil tracing with cues), increasing student responsibility while keeping supports aligned to the new motor demands. Section 4 appropriately raises cognitive complexity to phoneme-to-grapheme application in words; support does not drop to “low,” but the maintained scaffolding is justified by the harder integrated task (segmenting + mapping + writing). Within Section 4, the move from tracing to writing further supports the release arc.

However, the student task is only to write the beginning sound (c), so full mastery of the other phonemes is not strictly required to complete the directed task as modeled. If students must repeat/stretch /u/ or /p/ accurately, lack of review could raise difficulty for struggling learners, but it is not a clear missing decomposition step given the narrow writing demand. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 3–4 show strong logical scaffolding for the main composite demands: (1) producing correct C/c formation on lines and (2) using initial-sound listening to choose/write c. Letter formation is decomposed well: explicit modeling with stroke language in Section 2, motor rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) and line orientation (Finger Run) in Section 3, then tracing/writing in Section 4. Phonological demands are also scaffolded: /c/ is introduced with articulatory features and contrasted with /g/, then practiced in Sound Spark, and initial-sound focus is practiced in Sound Hunt with explicit stretching. The Application tasks are low-level per Form D, and the lesson includes a required think-aloud that models the full listen→stretch→identify first sound→write c routine. The only mild concern for a struggling group is that some application example words contain phonemes not reviewed in Section 1 (e.g., /u/, /p/, /n/), but because students are only asked to encode the first sound, the lesson’s decomposition is still largely adequate and does not clearly omit a prerequisite skill.

For Section 3 (motor task), the teacher demonstrates the full stroke sequence for uppercase C and lowercase c with verbal cues, and explicitly ties the letter to the /c/ sound. For Section 4 (cognitive task), the lesson includes explicit think-alouds using specific words (cat, cup, cap) where the teacher stretches phonemes, identifies the first sound, states the sound-to-letter match (“/c/ is written with c”), and writes the letter in the correct place before students attempt the tasks.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, nearly all included activities provide an explanation after the first and/or second elicited student response (e.g., linking correctness to articulatory features like quiet vs buzzy vocal box, matching beginning sound to word, and start-line differences in letter formation). The main gap is in the /c/ vs /g/ comparison sequence: after students produce /c/ with a hand on the throat, the teacher moves straight to producing /g/ without acknowledging/explaining that response (classified as nothing). Sound Spark in Section 1 was excluded per directions (rapid fluency drill), but other meaningful response opportunities generally include clear “why” feedback.

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson is highly script-specific: it provides frequent verbatim teacher talk (call-and-response, articulation coaching, corrective feedback, and handwriting stroke cues) plus explicit student responses with content (e.g., children say /c/, identify quiet vs buzzy, chant formation cues). **Judge Summary:** Coverage is strong for many choral sound responses and Sound Hunt actions. Gaps appear when prompts require BOTH saying and doing (practice_task) but the script records only one component—most often the action without the verbal cue (Magic Finger Write, tracing/writing letters) or the verbal sound without the directed action (hand on throat/vocal box). There are also a few missing child responses entirely for key directives (show pencil grip; write lowercase c in application). Several responses are too generic to verify (e.g., “or similar,” “move fingers in air,” “repeat motion and words,” “saying cue” without quoting the cue).

|---|----------|-----------|-----------|

| 2 | Sound-Letter Hunt (students listen for initial sound /t/ in words and write t only if /t/ is initial) | middle | Direct match to Form D Application ‘Sound-Letter Hunt’ (middle): students analyze phoneme position (beginning) in spoken words and produce a written grapheme response (write t or leave blank). |
| 3 | Brief check/explanation after each word (stretch together; confirm why wrote/didn't write; cross out incorrect t if needed) | middle | This is embedded formative feedback/error correction during Sound-Letter Hunt. It requires students to reflect on the phoneme analysis they just did; overall demand aligns with the ongoing middle-level task. |
| 4 | Write the Sound You Hear (teacher says phoneme; students write matching letter; mixed with previously taught sounds) | low | Direct match to Form D Application ‘Write the Sound You Hear’ (low): isolated phoneme-to-grapheme writing is a simpler linguistic task than analyzing a whole word for initial sound and deciding whether to write. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Teacher think-aloud/worked example modeling stretching word and writing t for initial /t/ (demonstration) | Sound-Letter Hunt (students listen for initial sound /t/ in words and write t only if /t/ is initial) | + | Low → middle. Appropriate shift from teacher modeling/observing to students performing the word-level sound identification + writing response. |
| 2 | Sound-Letter Hunt (students listen for initial sound /t/ in words and write t only if /t/ is initial) | Brief check/explanation after each word (stretch together; confirm why wrote/didn't write; cross out incorrect t if needed) | + | Middle → middle and fits Valid Non-Escalation Pattern #2 (corrective feedback after practice/assessment). The check and correction directly follow students’ responses within the task. |
| 3 | Brief check/explanation after each word (stretch together; confirm why wrote/didn't write; cross out incorrect t if needed) | Write the Sound You Hear (teacher says phoneme; students write matching letter; mixed with previously taught sounds) | - | This is a true difficulty drop (middle → low) that does not fit the listed non-escalation patterns. ‘Write the Sound You Hear’ is not a simpler response mode that depends on the prior, harder technique; it actually removes the word-level phoneme-position analysis and decision-making required in Sound-Letter Hunt. If the intent is consolidation, it would be better placed before Sound-Letter Hunt (phoneme→letter warm-up) or replaced with a harder follow-up (e.g., Mystery Word Builder or writing /t/ in CVC words). |

**Judge Summary:** Sections 1 and 3 show clear within-section scaffolding: Review uses a single middle-level listening task; Guided Practice appropriately moves from finger formation (low) to pencil tracing (middle) with well-placed corrective feedback (valid remediation interrupts). Section 2 mostly escalates appropriately from sound work to grapheme formation, with an acceptable brief ‘simpler’ reinforcement step (gesture/choral /t/) after the voicing contrast as intentional consolidation. Section 4 has one sequencing concern: it moves from the more complex word-based Sound-Letter Hunt (middle) down to isolated phoneme dictation (Write the Sound You Hear, low) without an explicit scaffold rationale; this reads as de-escalation rather than building in difficulty.
