| # | Earlier (Section) | Later (Section) | Builds? | Reasoning |
|---|-------------------|-----------------|---------|-----------|
| 1 | Sound Detective (identify beginning/middle/end sounds in known words; stretch words into phonemes) (Review & Connect) | Introduce /t/ + compare /t/ vs /d/ using mouth/throat cues (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sound Detective warms up the listening routine the lesson relies on: attending to a specified position in a word (beginning/end) and orally stretching words into sounds. That primes students to focus on careful phoneme-level listening when the new target /t/ is introduced and contrasted with /d/ (a finer-grained discrimination task). |
| 2 | Sound Detective (includes words like 'sat' with /t/ at end) (Review & Connect) | Sound Hunt (listen for /t/ at the beginning of words; respond with gesture) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Although Review is meant to use known sounds, the activity still rehearses locating a sound in a specific position. That positional awareness transfers directly to Sound Hunt, which narrows the task to a single target phoneme (/t/) in one position (initial). The move from open-ended identification (many possible sounds) to targeted detection (/t/ vs not /t/) is a reasonable scaffold. |
| 3 | Introduce /t/ and contrast with /d/ (voicing/no-buzz; tongue placement) + /t/ gesture (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound Hunt (identify /t/ at beginning of words) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | The articulation/voicing contrast gives children concrete sensory criteria for recognizing /t/, and the gesture provides an easy response mode. Those supports make the subsequent listening task (hearing /t/ in initial position) more accessible and accurate. |
| 4 | Sound Hunt (hear /t/ in words; especially initial-position focus) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Connect /t/ to grapheme T/t + teacher models formation with stroke cues and line placement (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sound Hunt establishes the phoneme identity (/t/) in spoken words first; then the lesson maps that stable sound to its grapheme (T/t). This is an appropriate sound→symbol progression before asking children to produce the letter in writing. |
| 5 | Teacher models uppercase T and lowercase t formation with verbal stroke cues and start-line placement (Model, Explain & Engage) | Magic Finger Write (air/board finger tracing with same cues) (Guided Practice) | + | Magic Finger Write is a direct, lower-stakes motor rehearsal of the exact formation and placement the teacher just demonstrated. It strengthens the motor plan before introducing tools (pencil) and paper demands. |
| 6 | Magic Finger Write (gross-motor rehearsal of strokes and start points) (Guided Practice) | Trace & Say (pencil tracing on dotted letters with same cues) (Guided Practice) | + | This is a clean difficulty increase: from large-motor/low-precision rehearsal to fine-motor pencil control while maintaining the same verbal cues and stroke sequence. That is classic handwriting scaffolding. |
| 7 | Explicit model of letter formation + differences between uppercase T and lowercase t (start at top vs middle) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound-Letter Hunt (hear beginning sound in words; write 't' if /t/ is initial) (Application) | + | Application requires coordinating phoneme identification with correct grapheme production on lines. Because students already (a) practiced recognizing /t/ in words (Sound Hunt) and (b) learned/practiced how to write t with correct placement (modeling + guided practice), they have the prerequisites to write t when they detect /t/ initially. |
| 8 | Trace & Say (repeated formation practice + corrective feedback on start points and crossbar placement) (Guided Practice) | Write the Sound You Hear (hear /t/; independently write 't') (Application) | + | Write the Sound You Hear removes the word context and asks for immediate sound→letter retrieval and production, which is only appropriate after students have stabilized formation through tracing and received specific feedback on common errors (starting point, crossbar location). |

**Judge Summary:** Across sections 1–4, the lesson shows a coherent increase in difficulty: (1) a general phoneme-position listening warm-up, (2) explicit establishment of the new phoneme /t/ including a supportive /t/ vs /d/ contrast, followed by targeted detection of /t/ in words, (3) a well-scaffolded handwriting progression from teacher model → finger rehearsal → pencil tracing with error correction, and (4) application tasks that integrate listening for /t/ with writing the grapheme t. Overall, later tasks depend on and are prepared by earlier activities in a pedagogically sound way.

#### Release: 100.0% (3/3 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | high | Frequent explicit modeling: “My turn. Listen to my sound: /t/.”; “Watch me closely while I write uppercase T. No one is writing yet.”; Teacher controls response timing: repeated “Get Ready… Go.”; Choral/whole-group responding: “Everyone, watch my mouth”; “Say it with me while you watch”; Teacher provides the full stroke script: “Big line down, little line across the top”; “Little line down, little line across the top”; Task is primarily perception/discrimination + observing teacher writing; students are not producing written work yet |
| Section 3: Guided Practice | medium | Students begin producing the formation (first with finger, then pencil) but under synchronized teacher direction: “Your turn. We will say it together…”; “Ready… Write and say… Go.”; Strong physical/verbal scaffolds: finger-tracing in air; then “dotted lines” to trace; explicit start-point prompts (“put your pencil point on the top line where the first dotted T begins”); Inline monitoring and correction: “teacher circulates, correcting formation and starting points”; Error-focused re-modeling with contrasts: teacher draws incorrect vs correct T/t and leads a fix-up trace “with me” |
| Section 4: Application | low | Students must decide whether to write based on phoneme identification: “Decide if that beginning sound is /t/, and… If it is /t/, you will write the letter t… If you do not hear /t/… leave your line blank”; Independent trial embedded: “Now try one on your own… ‘mat’… Go.”; Reduced formation scaffolds (no dotted tracing mentioned; students write on “lines where you will write just one letter”); Teacher support shifts to checking/brief feedback after attempts rather than step-by-step formation prompting |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3: Guided Practice | + | Productive responsibility shifts from teacher demonstration (“No one is writing yet”) to students forming the letter (finger writing, then tracing). Even though Section 3 still uses heavy prompting, it is a clear move from I do to we do: students act, teacher coaches. |
| 2 | Section 3: Guided Practice | Section 4: Application | + | Section 4 removes key physical scaffolds (no dotted templates specified) and adds independent decision-making about whether /t/ is present, including an explicit independent item (“try one on your own”). Cognitive load increases (phoneme discrimination + conditional writing), but support appropriately shifts from formation-by-formation guidance to task-level directions and post-checking—an appropriate release. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1: Magic Finger Write (uppercase then lowercase) | high | Teacher leads stroke-by-stroke with a clear model: “My turn first. Watch…”; Choral, synchronized practice: “We will say it together as we move our fingers”; No tool demand (finger in the air): “No pencils yet”; Frequent repetition with teacher pacing: “Again. Ready… Go.” |
| 2 | Section 3 Step 2: Trace & Say (uppercase dotted T then lowercase dotted t, with corrective feedback) | medium | Students create permanent marks with a pencil: “We will trace and say… (Children trace…)”; Physical scaffold remains strong: “dotted lines” / tracing templates; Start-point and placement prompts: “put your pencil point on the top line…”; “Put your pencil on the middle line…”; Teacher monitoring and immediate correction: “teacher circulates, correcting”; re-teach sequences after errors; “trace one more… with me” |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1: Magic Finger Write (uppercase then lowercase) | Section 3 Step 2: Trace & Say (uppercase dotted T then lowercase dotted t, with corrective feedback) | + | Although the dotted template adds physical guidance, the step introduces a new fine-motor demand (pencil control) and increases productive responsibility (students make actual marks). The dotted lines appropriately offset the added motor load, so overall this is a valid release toward real writing. |

**Judge Summary:** The lesson shows a coherent gradual release arc from Section 2→3→4. Section 2 is strongly teacher-led with explicit modeling of articulation, gesture, and letter formation while students mostly observe/chorally respond. Section 3 moves students into producing the letter with high structure: gross-motor finger rehearsal followed by pencil-based Trace & Say on dotted lines, including corrective feedback—appropriate guided practice. Section 4 shifts the responsibility further: students must listen for /t/, decide whether to write, and write t on lines without tracing, including explicit independent trials. Any continued teacher scripting in Section 4 functions as task-structure support for a more cognitively complex sound-to-letter application, consistent with gradual release rather than a regression.

Section 1 reviews known sounds at the phoneme level, supporting discrimination. **Judge Summary:** Sections 3–4 are logically scaffolded from (1) sound awareness and segmentation strategy (Section 1 Sound Detective), to (2) explicit phoneme articulation/discrimination and initial-position listening for /t/ (Section 2), plus direct modeling of T/t stroke sequences with line placement, to (3) supported motor rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) and controlled pencil tracing with corrective feedback (Trace & Say), and finally to (4) integrating listening + letter choice + writing in Sound-Letter Hunt and Write the Sound You Hear. No critical component skill is missing for the Section 4 tasks as written. The only potential vulnerability is that independent (non-traced) lowercase t writing is assumed after tracing; however, Magic Finger Write + Trace & Say + the guided ‘write t with me’ in Application provides sufficient explicit lead-in for kindergarten expectations.

**Judge Summary:** The lesson includes two clear corrective-feedback blocks that (1) anticipate specific, realistic kindergarten handwriting errors for uppercase T and lowercase t and (2) provide scripted teacher talk with modeling of incorrect vs. correct forms. In both cases, the script explains the distinguishing feature (start point for uppercase T; crossbar placement/touching the stem for lowercase t) and actively engages students by asking them to compare examples and then immediately retry the correct formation. Elsewhere, there are a few moments of general monitoring/correction (e.g., “teacher circulates, correcting formation”), but the two labeled blocks are the only fully rubric-aligned, scripted corrective feedback.

A few follow-ups are confirmation-only (e.g., after children say /t/ the teacher says only “Good,” and some handwriting start-point answers get only “Yes…” with no added rationale). **Judge Summary:** The lesson includes a strong phoneme comparison of /t/ vs /d/ with explicit similarity (same tongue placement), difference (voicing/buzz vs no-buzz), clear articulatory cues (tongue behind top teeth, hand on throat/vocal box, puff of air), and students producing both sounds repeatedly. It also includes an explicit letter-form comparison of uppercase T vs lowercase t (same strokes vs different size/starting line), but that letter comparison is mostly visual/verbal analysis and partner talk; it does not explicitly require students to form/write both letters as a contrast task within that specific comparison moment (though both are practiced elsewhere in the lesson).

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson is largely verbatim and highly usable as a read-aloud script: frequent teacher talk with explicit sound articulation guidance (mouth/throat check), gesture support for /t/, and sequenced stroke cues for uppercase/lowercase T/t. Pacing/management cues (“My turn… Your turn… Get Ready… Go.”) are consistently embedded. The main places requiring improvisation are the bracketed/brace notes where the teacher is told to ‘continue with more words/sounds’ or ‘monitor/circulate/correct grips’ without providing the exact next lines; Section 4 also contains one generic child-response description (“many will not write anything”). Overall, the rubric criterion (#10) is met strongly: detailed explanations, pronunciation practice with tactile cues, gesture to cement sound, and explicit, repeatable letter-formation verbal sequences.

Sub-metrics: Present: 92.7% 38/41 | Specific: 78.9% 30/38 | Complete: 46.3% 19/41

**Judge Summary:** Sections 1–4 contain many interaction points with specific verbal responses for phoneme ID and many combined action+verbal responses during Magic Finger Write and some tracing/writing. Gaps occur mainly when the script gives a directive/practice task but fails to record the child action (e.g., “put your pencil point on…,” “put your pencil on the middle line,” “cross it out”), and when it records only speech but not the required writing/tracing action (several Trace & Say corrective ‘Ready… Go’ lines). In Sound Hunt non-/t/ items, responses often specify ‘no gesture’ but do not clearly specify what children say (e.g., ‘no’ vs silence), even though the teacher asks a yes/no question. In Application, the ‘mat’ item is treated like a question but the provided response describes writing behavior rather than the expected spoken answer (/m/ or ‘no’).

**Review & Connect**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Sort the sound (continuous vs stop; mark circle/X under given letters) | high | Matches Form D Review & Connect ‘Sort the sound’ (high): sorting sounds by voiced/unvoiced or continuous/stops. |
| 2 | Write the sound (teacher says phoneme; students write matching letter) | high | Matches Form D Review & Connect ‘Write the sound’ (high): recall phoneme→grapheme and produce written letter. |
| 3 | How many sounds (phoneme count with taps/Elkonin boxes) | high | Matches Form D Review & Connect ‘How many sounds’ (high): segment/count phonemes in a spoken word using manipulatives. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Sort the sound (continuous vs stop; mark circle/X under given letters) | Write the sound (teacher says phoneme; students write matching letter) | + | High→high. Although both are high, ‘Write the sound’ shifts from classification to phoneme-grapheme retrieval + letter production, which is at least as demanding as sorting. |
| 2 | Write the sound (teacher says phoneme; students write matching letter) | How many sounds (phoneme count with taps/Elkonin boxes) | + | High→high. Both are advanced; moving to explicit phoneme counting/segmenting maintains or increases cognitive demand (phonemic segmentation across the whole word vs single sound-to-letter mapping). |

|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce /w/ with gesture and articulatory comparison to /v/ (partner discussion; voiced check) | middle | Not named in Form D; best match is explicit phoneme articulation/discrimination instruction (harder than simple ‘Sound Spark’ production, but not as complex as Sound Puzzle/Switch-a-Roo manipulation). Adds metalinguistic comparison (/w/ vs /v/) and partner explanation. |
| 2 | Sound Puzzle (add /w/ to known phonemes to build/blend words) | high | Matches Form D Model/Explain/Engage ‘Sound Puzzle’ (high): combine given phonemes including target to create/blend words. |

| 4 | Connect sound to letter Ww + teacher models stroke sequence/placement (uppercase then lowercase) | middle | Handwriting modeling with verbal cues is foundational and teacher-led; it resembles introducing formation before students write. It is more demanding than simple sound production but less demanding than students independently writing (as in Top to Bottom Challenge/Cover & Try). |
| 5 | Corrective Feedback Block #1 (contrast incorrect vs correct W; partner compare; air-write fix) | middle | This is targeted error analysis and reteach of formation features (start line, slant strokes). Fits the rubric’s ‘corrective feedback after assessment/attempt’ type of step; cognitive load is moderate and supports accuracy before practice. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce /w/ with gesture and articulatory comparison to /v/ (partner discussion; voiced check) | Sound Puzzle (add /w/ to known phonemes to build/blend words) | + | Middle→high. Students move from producing/discriminating /w/ to blending/constructing words with multiple phonemes including /w/, which is more complex. |
| 2 | Sound Puzzle (add /w/ to known phonemes to build/blend words) | Sound Switch-a-Roo (substitute phonemes to /w/ or /v/; produce new words) | + | High→high and typically increasing: substitution/manipulation (Switch-a-Roo) is at least as complex as blending given phonemes (Sound Puzzle). |
| 3 | Sound Switch-a-Roo (substitute phonemes to /w/ or /v/; produce new words) | Connect sound to letter Ww + teacher models stroke sequence/placement (uppercase then lowercase) | - | High→middle without a listed valid non-escalation pattern. This is a real downshift from complex phoneme manipulation to teacher-led letter formation modeling. While it may be logical for lesson flow (phonology → grapheme/handwriting), it is not ordered from simpler to more complex within the section. |
| 4 | Connect sound to letter Ww + teacher models stroke sequence/placement (uppercase then lowercase) | Corrective Feedback Block #1 (contrast incorrect vs correct W; partner compare; air-write fix) | + | Middle→middle. Feedback directly follows initial formation modeling/likely quick student attempts/observations and is an appropriate ‘planned remediation interrupt’ (Valid Pattern #2), not an escalation issue. |

Because it is teacher-led and students are not yet generating independently, it is less demanding than student execution. |
| 2 | Mystery Word Builder – student practice (wet, win; segment, write each phoneme’s letter, then read) | middle | Matches Form D Application ‘Mystery Word Builder’ (middle): students hear phonemes sequentially, write corresponding graphemes to build a word, then blend/read. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|

Responsibility shifts from teacher modeling to student performance; same task type but increased learner demand (appropriate gradual release). |
| 2 | Mystery Word Builder – student practice (wet, win; segment, write each phoneme’s letter, then read) | Sound Switch Writing (write win, then change one phoneme/grapheme to make wen; read) | + | Middle→high. Students move from straightforward phoneme-by-phoneme encoding to rewriting and manipulating one phoneme/grapheme while holding the rest constant—more complex. |
| 3 | Sound Switch Writing (write win, then change one phoneme/grapheme to make wen; read) | What’s that Pattern (segment words, count phonemes with chips, write number of sounds; notice shared 3-sound pattern) | + | High→high. Both are high-level phonemic tasks; ‘What’s that Pattern’ sustains advanced segmentation/counting and adds pattern generalization across words, so it is not a reduction in complexity. |

**Judge Summary:** Sections 1, 3, and 4 are largely well-sequenced (generally steady or increasing demand, with acceptable high→middle drops only when used as corrective-feedback remediation). Section 2 shows one sequencing concern: after two high-level phonemic manipulation activities (Sound Puzzle → Sound Switch-a-Roo), the section drops to mid-level teacher-led letter-formation modeling. That transition is logical for lesson flow (phonology to grapheme/handwriting), but it is not an within-section escalation from simpler to more complex per the rubric.

#### Across: 100.0% (11/11 links)

| # | Earlier (Section) | Later (Section) | Builds? | Reasoning |
|---|-------------------|-----------------|---------|-----------|
| 1 | How many sounds (Review & Connect) | Sound Puzzle (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Section 1’s phoneme-counting routine (tapping/Elkonin boxes for 3-phoneme words) warms up attention to discrete phonemes and left-to-right sound sequencing. That primes Section 2’s Sound Puzzle, which requires holding individual phonemes in memory and blending them in order—including inserting the new /w/ phoneme into a known CVC frame. |
| 2 | Write the sound (Review & Connect) | Introduce /w/ sound and compare with /v/ (articulatory comparison) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Although Section 1 does not include /w/, it rehearses the core routine of hearing a phoneme and producing its grapheme (plus quick articulatory talk for /v/). That prepares students to attend to mouth features and then anchor the new /w/ against a confusable, previously learned /v/ when /w/ is introduced. |
| 3 | Sort the sound (continuous vs stop) (Review & Connect) | Introduce /w/ sound and compare with /v/ (articulatory comparison) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sort the sound cues children to listen to how a sound is produced (can it be held, what does the mouth/voice do). That general ‘feature noticing’ routine supports the later articulatory comparison of /w/ versus /v/ (lips rounded vs teeth-lip contact; both voiced), which depends on careful phoneme discrimination. |
| 4 | Introduce /w/ sound and compare with /v/ (gesture + articulatory features) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound Switch-a-Roo (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sound Switch-a-Roo is higher cognitive load: students must manipulate phonemes and keep contrasts stable while swapping. The explicit /w/ vs /v/ comparison immediately beforehand strengthens discrimination so children can successfully swap /w/→/v/ (win→vin) and attend to ‘only the first sound changed.’ |
| 5 | Sound Puzzle (Model, Explain & Engage) | Mystery Word Builder (Application) | + | Sound Puzzle provides oral practice blending sequences that include /w/ in CVC/CV strings. Mystery Word Builder then adds the next layer: segmenting those same kinds of words and writing the graphemes (including w) in order in boxes/lines. The earlier blending with /w/ supports accurate phoneme sequencing and positional awareness for encoding. |
| 6 | Sound Switch-a-Roo (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound Switch Writing (Application) | + | Both tasks involve phoneme substitution; the earlier activity is oral (manipulate sounds), while the later requires applying the substitution to print (erase/replace the grapheme). The oral switching practice is a direct prerequisite for successful written switching, reducing cognitive load when children must change only one position. |
| 7 | Connect /w/ to Ww and model letter formation (stroke sequence + line placement) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Top to Bottom Challenge (Guided Practice) | + | The teacher explicitly models uppercase W starting point, stroke order, and verbal cues. Top to Bottom Challenge is the same motor plan but transferred to student production with self-checks for starting at the top and correct placement—an appropriate release from demonstration to supported practice. |
| 8 | Connect /w/ to Ww and model letter formation (stroke sequence + line placement) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Line Talkers (Guided Practice) | + | Line Talkers depends on having a clear model of where the letter starts and what lines it touches. The prior modeling explicitly contrasts uppercase (top-to-bottom) with lowercase (middle-to-bottom), enabling children to verbalize and self-monitor line placement during guided practice. |
| 9 | Connect /w/ to Ww and model letter formation + corrective feedback on common errors (Model, Explain & Engage) | Cover & Try (Guided Practice) | + | Cover & Try requires internalizing the motor sequence and retrieving it without a visible guide. The earlier explicit stroke script and error-correction (slants, correct start line) build the mental model children must rely on when the dotted exemplar is covered. |
| 10 | Top to Bottom Challenge / Line Talkers / Cover & Try (forming Ww with correct strokes and placement) (Guided Practice) | Mystery Word Builder (Application) | + | Mystery Word Builder requires children to write w quickly and accurately as part of spelling CVC words. Guided practice establishes legible, automatized formation and line placement so the handwriting demand doesn’t overwhelm the spelling/phoneme-mapping demand in Application. |
| 11 | How many sounds (Review & Connect) | What’s that Pattern (Application) | + | Section 1 introduces the ‘tap/box per phoneme’ routine. Section 4’s What’s that Pattern repeats and extends that exact phoneme-counting skill with new /w/ words, asking children to segment and quantify phonemes (a direct continuation rather than a new task type). |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson shows strong scaffolding from phonological warm-ups (sound feature attention, phoneme counting, and sound→letter retrieval with known sounds) into explicit introduction/discrimination of the new /w/ (including /w/ vs /v/), then into advanced oral phoneme manipulation with /w/ (Sound Puzzle/Switch-a-Roo), followed by clear modeling of Ww formation. Guided Practice appropriately shifts from teacher model to supported student production and then memory-based production (Cover & Try). Application then integrates the two strands—phoneme work and handwriting—by requiring students to segment/encode /w/ words and perform written sound switching, which is well-primed by earlier oral switching and letter-formation practice. Overall, difficulty increases logically and component skills are practiced before being combined.

#### Release: 75.0% (3/4 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2 (Model, Explain & Engage) | high | Teacher-led articulation/gesture routine with explicit prompts: “My turn. Listen: /w/… Your turn: Get ready… Go.”; Teacher models and contrasts /w/ vs /v/ with explicit directions: “Look at my mouth and listen…” then explains differences; High teacher structuring of phonemic tasks: “My turn first… {Teacher writes: /w/ /e/ /t/}… Listen as I put them together…”; Teacher explicitly models letter formation with attention control: “Pencils are down. Eyes on my hand.”; Stroke-by-stroke verbal scripting and choral rehearsal: “Start at the top line. Slant down. Slant up…” with call-and-response “Say it with me…”; Inline corrective feedback with re-model and guided air-write: “Some of you… watch… Let’s fix it together. Everyone air-write…” |
| Section 3 (Guided Practice) | medium | Explicit transfer to student performance but with teacher prompting: “Now it’s your turn to write uppercase W…”; Teacher provides start-point/placement cues and monitoring: “Put your pencil at the top line… Let me see. {Teacher scans and adjusts.}”; Choral procedural scaffold remains during writing: “We are going to write and say together… ‘Slant down, slant up…’”; Use of self-check criteria provided by teacher: “Did your W touch the top line and the bottom line? Put a little star…”; Cover & Try includes a scaffolded sequence (trace → cover → write) and comparison checklist: “compare… Is your w starting at the middle… Does it have four slant lines?”; Corrective feedback block still includes teacher model and a guided fix: “Everyone look… Let’s fix it… write one v… add another v…” |
| Section 4 (Application) | low | Task shifts from single-letter formation to encoding/word-building (CVC): children must segment, select graphemes, and write multiple letters (“write the three letters in your boxes and then on the handwriting line”); After one teacher think-aloud, students write with less formation scripting: “Ready… Write. Go. (Children write w-i-n.)”; Less stroke-by-stroke handwriting cueing; prompts focus on phoneme→grapheme decisions rather than how to form each letter; Students execute partial sequences more independently (sound switch): “Erase just the middle letter and write the new letter…”; Pattern task requires independent segmentation/counting and recording: “Stretch it, tap it, then write how many sounds… (Children… writing 3.)” |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2 (Model, Explain & Engage) | Section 3 (Guided Practice) | + | The lesson moves from teacher modeling and choral rehearsal (“Pencils are down. Eyes on my hand… Watch me closely”) to student production on paper (“Now it’s your turn to write uppercase W”). The teacher still prompts and monitors, but productive responsibility (writing W/w on their own paper) clearly increases. |
| 2 | Section 3 (Guided Practice) | Section 4 (Application) | + | Section 4 introduces a higher cognitive demand (encoding CVC words, sound switching, counting phonemes). The teacher keeps some structural supports (Elkonin boxes; stepwise prompts), but removes much of the handwriting-procedure scaffolding (less ‘write-and-say’ stroke scripting) and expects children to apply letter-sound knowledge and write multiple-letter sequences. This is a valid release via cognitive-load trade-off: increased task complexity with comparatively reduced formation support. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | 3a. Top to Bottom Challenge – uppercase W (write-and-say with teacher monitoring) | medium | Teacher sets start location and checks it: “Where do we start…?… Put your pencil at the top line… {Teacher scans and adjusts.}”; Choral procedural cue while writing: “We are going to write and say together… Write and say. Go.”; Teacher-provided success criteria for self-check: “Did your W touch the top line and the bottom line? Put a little star…” |
| 2 | 3b. Line Talkers – describe line placement for W/w | medium | Teacher models description first: “Look at my W… My W starts at the top line…”; Students respond with a prompted self-script: “Whisper to yourself… ‘My W starts…’”; Teacher calls on an individual and confirms: “Now, who can tell me…?… Yes, lowercase w starts…” |
| 3 | 3c. Cover & Try – trace lowercase w → cover model → write from memory → compare | low | Initial physical scaffold (dotted lowercase w): “trace the lowercase w that I have dotted for you”; Then scaffold is removed for production: “cover… try to write lowercase w without looking at the dots”; Students self-evaluate against criteria: “compare… Circle your best one.” |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | 3a. Top to Bottom Challenge – uppercase W (write-and-say with teacher monitoring) | 3b. Line Talkers – describe line placement for W/w | - | Support does not clearly decrease. The task shifts from motor production (writing) to metalinguistic description (talking about line placement). Teacher still models first (“Look at my W…”) and prompts a scripted student response. Because the response remains highly prompted and the cognitive demand changes, this reads as a lateral move (different skill focus) rather than a release. |
| 2 | 3b. Line Talkers – describe line placement for W/w | 3c. Cover & Try – trace lowercase w → cover model → write from memory → compare | + | Even though 3c begins with tracing (a physical scaffold), it quickly removes the model for the key attempt (“cover… write… without looking”). Compared with Line Talkers (teacher model + prompted oral script), Cover & Try requires independent recall of formation and places evaluation on the student (“compare… circle your best”). The dotted trace offsets added motor demand only at the start; overall independence increases. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 2→3→4 show a coherent gradual release: Section 2 is teacher-heavy modeling (articulation, phonemic manipulation demos, and explicit stroke scripting), Section 3 shifts to student handwriting with guided cues and monitoring, and Section 4 asks students to apply the /w/ mapping in more complex spelling tasks with relatively less formation scaffolding. Within Section 3, the clearest release occurs in Cover & Try (trace then write from memory). The only weak point is the 3a→3b sequence, which is more of a lateral shift (writing to describing) than a clear decrease in teacher support; however, 3c re-establishes the downward support trajectory by removing the visual model and requiring independent formation.

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 3–4 are logically scaffolded and the main complex application demands (encode CVC words with /w/; perform sound substitution in writing; segment/count phonemes) have their component sub-skills taught earlier. The lesson explicitly teaches: (1) phoneme segmentation/counting in Section 1 (How Many Sounds), (2) phoneme substitution orally in Section 2 (Sound Switch-a-Roo), (3) the /w/↔Ww correspondence plus detailed stroke sequence and placement in Section 2, and (4) supported-to-independent letter formation in Section 3 (Top to Bottom Challenge, Cover & Try) with corrective feedback for the w/v confusion. Application tasks then combine these skills appropriately, and any non-target letters required are reasonably covered by prior instruction and partial review (Write the sound), which the prompt allows as previously mastered.

**Judge Summary:** The lesson meets the detailed modeling/think-aloud criterion for both sections that require independent student work. **Judge Summary:** The lesson includes two explicit corrective feedback blocks. Each anticipates a specific, realistic kindergarten error (incorrect stroke/starting point for uppercase W; confusing w with v or producing an incomplete w), provides scripted teacher language, explains how to distinguish correct vs. incorrect formation (top-line start, all slants, four slant strokes), and actively engages students through comparison questions, partner talk, and guided repair (air-writing and constructing w from two v shapes).

**Judge Summary:** Yes—multiple explicit comparisons are present that meet Criteria #6 and #11. The lesson directly compares the target phoneme /w/ with confusable /v/ using articulatory features (lip rounding vs teeth-on-lip, voicing), has students produce both sounds, and revisits /w/ vs /v/ through phoneme substitution (Switch-a-Roo). It also compares the letter forms v and w during corrective feedback, explicitly analyzing shared slant features and the key difference (2 slants vs 4) and requiring students to write both.

Sub-metrics: Dialogue (vol-adj): 87.4% | Examples: 15 words | Responses: 100.0% | Pacing: 100.0%

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson is highly script-specific and strongly meets Criterion #10: it includes ample verbatim teacher language, explicit articulatory instructions (hand in front of mouth; lip rounding vs teeth-on-lip for /v/), a memorable gesture for /w/, and clear, sequenced stroke-formation verbal cues for uppercase/lowercase Ww (“Slant down, slant up…” tied to line placement). Pacing/choral-response routines (“My turn… Your turn… Get ready… Go.”) are frequent and consistent. The main places requiring teacher improvisation are the few brace-style continuations (e.g., “Teacher continues briefly…”), and occasional monitoring directions (scan/adjust), but these are limited and do not undermine overall script usability.

Sub-metrics: Present: 83.0% 39/47 | Specific: 89.7% 35/39 | Complete: 53.2% 25/47

**Judge Summary:** Most interaction points include some child response, and many are specific (especially choral phoneme/word responses). The main gaps are: (1) several directives/practice prompts lack any recorded child action (e.g., show pinch grip, place pencil, cover model, lift cover/compare); (2) multiple practice tasks provide only the action (writing/tapping) but omit the verbal component even when the teacher says “write and say” or when saying sounds/labels would make the response verifiable; (3) partner-talk prompts in both corrective feedback blocks have no scripted child response; (4) a few responses are too generic (e.g., “Children whisper,” “Children write,” “air-write W with cues”) and should specify exactly what children say/do.

_No consecutive pairs (0 or 1 activity)_

**Model, Explain & Engage**
| # | Activity | Difficulty | Reasoning |
|---|----------|-----------|-----------|
| 1 | Introduce new /a/ phoneme with mouth formation + chin-tap gesture (students repeat) | low | Primarily sound production/phoneme awareness with imitation and gesture support; closest to Form D ‘Sound Spark’/‘Sound Hunt’ style tasks (low) where children hear/produce the target sound without manipulating phonemes. |
| 2 | Compare /a/ vs confusable /o/ using mouth-shape contrast and student trials | middle | Requires phoneme discrimination and articulatory feature comparison across two similar vowels; not explicitly listed, but cognitively beyond simple repetition (low) and aligns with a mid-level auditory discrimination task. |
| 3 | Connect /a/ to letter name/symbols A and a (identify/point to uppercase vs lowercase; say /a/ with gesture) | low | Letter identification/association with a supported response (pointing + choral /a/) is simpler than phoneme manipulation; comparable to low-demand recognition/production tasks in Form D (e.g., Sound Spark/Tap that Sound style response modes). |
| 4 | Chop-a-Sound with /a/ in CVC/VC words (remove one phoneme and say what remains) | middle | Direct match to Form D Model, Explain & Engage ‘Chop-a-Sound’ (middle): children manipulate phonemes by deleting/chopping and producing the remainder. |
| 5 | Teacher models uppercase A formation with verbal stroke cues; students verbally rehearse strokes and count lines | low | This is teacher modeling + oral rehearsal without pencil (a low-demand rehearsal step, similar in spirit to Guided Practice ‘Rehearsal of sequence of strokes’ low-level activities). |
| 6 | Teacher models lowercase a formation with verbal stroke cues; students verbally rehearse strokes | low | Same rationale as uppercase modeling: observation + oral rehearsal of stroke sequence without independent writing is low cognitive/motor demand. |
| 7 | Compare uppercase A vs lowercase a (discuss what is same/different, especially start point) | middle | Requires metalinguistic/metacognitive comparison of two forms and articulating differences (start point/size), which is more complex than simple imitation/pointing but not as complex as phoneme manipulation + writing. |

|---|------|----|----------|-----------|

| 2 | Compare /a/ vs confusable /o/ using mouth-shape contrast and student trials | Connect /a/ to letter name/symbols A and a (identify/point to uppercase vs lowercase; say /a/ with gesture) | - | Middle → low drop: after a higher-demand discrimination/contrast task, the lesson shifts to a simpler identification/pointing routine. This does not clearly fit the allowed ‘simpler response format’ pattern because the later task does not require using the /a/ vs /o/ contrast skill; it’s mostly a new linkage to graphemes rather than practice of the comparison skill. |
| 3 | Connect /a/ to letter name/symbols A and a (identify/point to uppercase vs lowercase; say /a/ with gesture) | Chop-a-Sound with /a/ in CVC/VC words (remove one phoneme and say what remains) | + | Low → middle: moves from basic sound-letter introduction/recognition to phoneme manipulation (Form D Chop-a-Sound), which is more complex. |

It functions as a scaffolded bridge into handwriting rather than a new, unrelated low task. |

The comparison and correction is more demanding than finger tracing, but the retry remains in the Trace & Say supported format (middle). It aligns with Form D’s notion that more reflective formation attention is higher than low-level rehearsal but not independent writing (high). |

Difficulty does not need to increase; the feedback is a planned remediation interrupt that supports accuracy before continuing. |

| 2 | Students complete Sound-Letter Hunt across multiple words (write A/a if /a/ initial; write nothing if not) | middle | Same Form D ‘Sound-Letter Hunt’ (middle). Students must listen for beginning sound and produce the grapheme in the correct spot; response is writing (even if on dotted lines), making it more than low-level tracing. |
| 3 | Corrective Feedback Block 2: fix confusions (writing m or o for /a/) with sound/letter contrast and correction on paper | middle | Targeted error-correction and letter-sound contrast following an application attempt; consistent with remediation/feedback demands rather than introducing a harder new task. |

| # | From | To | Ordered? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Sound-Letter Hunt teacher think-aloud/worked example (listen for beginning /a/ in a word, then write A; model stretching word and matching sound to letter) | Students complete Sound-Letter Hunt across multiple words (write A/a if /a/ initial; write nothing if not) | + | Modeled example → independent/participatory practice is an appropriate scaffold; underlying task remains middle but student cognitive load increases appropriately. |
| 2 | Students complete Sound-Letter Hunt across multiple words (write A/a if /a/ initial; write nothing if not) | Corrective Feedback Block 2: fix confusions (writing m or o for /a/) with sound/letter contrast and correction on paper | + | Correctly ordered by Valid Non-Escalation Pattern 2 (corrective feedback after assessment/practice): feedback follows observed errors in the application task and supports improved performance. |

**Judge Summary:** Sections 1, 3, and 4 show solid within-section scaffolding: Review & Connect stays at a single (middle) level; Guided Practice properly escalates from Magic Finger Write (low) to Trace & Say (middle) and then uses a justified feedback interrupt; Application moves from teacher modeling to student practice with a justified feedback interrupt. Section 2 is mostly scaffolded from low → middle → (back to low) → middle → handwriting modeling (low) → comparison (middle). The one notable sequencing concern is the drop from /a/ vs /o/ comparison (middle) to simple letter pointing/association (low) without an explicit ‘apply the comparison’ step; otherwise the flow is pedagogically coherent.

Section 2 then applies that same beginning-sound listening routine to the new target phoneme /a/, so children are cognitively warmed up for the same type of task with new content. |
| 2 | Sound Detective (point to the matching sound card after hearing the beginning phoneme) (Review & Connect) | Connect /a/ to letter name/graphemes A/a (point to big A/little a; 'Letter A says /a/') (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Pointing to a sound card after hearing the beginning sound is an easier, recognition-based mapping step (sound → known symbol). Section 2 extends that mapping to the new target by explicitly linking the heard sound /a/ to its written symbols A/a, preserving the same 'hear sound → select/locate symbol' routine before asking children to produce the writing. |
| 3 | Introduce /a/ sound with mouth/gesture cues and contrast with /o/ (Model, Explain & Engage) | Chop-a-Sound with words containing /a/ (e.g., am, at, an) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Chop-a-Sound requires children to hold phonemes in mind and manipulate them (remove /t/ or /n/ to leave /a/). The earlier explicit articulatory/gesture cueing and the /a/ vs /o/ contrast strengthen phoneme clarity and discrimination, which is a prerequisite for successful phoneme manipulation in Chop-a-Sound. |
| 4 | Chop-a-Sound (isolating /a/ as what's left when other phonemes are removed) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound-Letter Hunt (listen for beginning /a/ and write A/a; write nothing if not /a/) (Application) | + | Chop-a-Sound practices attending to /a/ within simple CVC/VC structures and confirms /a/ can be isolated as a single sound. Sound-Letter Hunt then asks children to use that auditory isolation skill specifically at the beginning position and make the decision to mark it in print (or inhibit writing when /a/ is absent), which is a harder, applied form of the same phoneme-attention skill. |
| 5 | Teacher models uppercase A and lowercase a formation with explicit verbal stroke cues and start points (Model, Explain & Engage) | Magic Finger Write (uppercase A then lowercase a using the same cues) (Guided Practice) | + | The modeled formation provides the visual + verbal motor plan (stroke sequence and placement). Magic Finger Write is a lower-stakes rehearsal that directly uses that motor plan without tool demands, appropriately bridging from teacher demonstration to child production. |
| 6 | Magic Finger Write (gross-motor/kinesthetic rehearsal of strokes on lines) (Guided Practice) | Trace & Say (pencil tracing on dotted letters while verbalizing strokes) (Guided Practice) | + | Magic Finger Write builds spatial orientation to the lines and internalizes the stroke sequence. Trace & Say increases difficulty by adding fine-motor control with a writing tool while maintaining the same verbal cues, so the progression is properly scaffolded. |
| 7 | Trace & Say for A/a with start-point checks and corrective feedback (e.g., 'little a starts at the middle') (Guided Practice) | Sound-Letter Hunt (write A/a on dotted lines when /a/ is heard at beginning) (Application) | + | Application requires coordinating phonemic decision-making with letter production. Trace & Say ensures children can execute the letterforms with correct start points and stroke sequences (and includes error correction), which reduces motor/cognitive load so children can focus on the sound-to-letter match during Sound-Letter Hunt. |
| 8 | Explicit sound-to-letter link: 'Letter A says /a/' + pointing to uppercase/lowercase (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound-Letter Hunt (choose to write A or a when hearing /a/) (Application) | + | Sound-Letter Hunt depends on a stable phoneme–grapheme correspondence for the target: hearing /a/ → retrieving A/a. Section 2 establishes that mapping clearly (sound, name, symbols), enabling children to apply it in a writing context in Section 4. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson generally follows a logical scaffold from easier auditory identification routines (beginning-sound detection with known sounds) to explicit teaching of the new target sound /a/ (including articulatory cues and contrast), then to phoneme manipulation (Chop-a-Sound), then to letter-name/grapheme connection and modeled formation, then to supported motor practice (Magic Finger Write → Trace & Say), and finally to application that integrates listening and writing (Sound-Letter Hunt with write/no-write decisions). The links are substantive and mostly increase in cognitive/motor demand in an appropriate sequence for PreK.

#### Release: 50.0% (3/6 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | high | Repeated teacher-led cues: “My turn: listen to my sound… /a/ /a/ /a/.”; Teacher models mouth/gesture and directs choral imitation: “Everyone, watch my mouth… Now put your hand under your chin and say /a/.”; Teacher leads comparison explicitly: “My turn: /a/. My turn: /o/… Say /a/… Say /o/.”; Teacher provides worked example for phoneme manipulation: “Watch me first. My turn. The word is am… If I chop off /a/, what is left?”; Teacher explicitly models letter formation with micro-steps: “Eyes on me. No pencils yet. Just watching… Big line up, big line down, little line across.”; Children mainly respond chorally/receptively (“Say it with me while you watch…”, “Everyone say it with me…”) |
| Section 3: Guided Practice | medium | Teacher frames it as shared practice: “Now it’s your turn to practice writing letter A with me.”; High physical/verbal scaffolds remain: “No pencils yet. Just your magic finger.” and later “Trace & Say” with dotted letters and “start dot.”; Teacher continues step-by-step prompting and choral cueing: “Get ready… Big line up… Big line down… Little line across.”; “Write and say. Ready… go.”; Teacher actively monitors and corrects: “Let me see your fingers ready… Teacher checks and gently adjusts.”; “Let me check… Teacher scans, corrects a few.”; Built-in reteach/correction block with explicit contrast modeling: “Some of our little a’s are starting up at the top… Watch… This is the right way.” |
| Section 4: Application | low | Task shifts to children applying skill to a new context: “Now your turn… Listen for the beginning sound… If you hear /a/… write letter A or a… If not… do not write anything.”; Children must decide whether to write (conditional response), not just trace on command (e.g., “dog… So we do not write A or a this time.”); Teacher circulation is monitoring rather than co-writing: “(children write; teacher circulates)”; Students write on worksheet lines during the activity (“write A or a on the dotted lines”), with less stroke-by-stroke narration from teacher during each item; Although a worked example is provided, subsequent items are student-executed with brief prompts rather than continuous micro-step formation coaching |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2: Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3: Guided Practice | + | Section 2 is primarily teacher demonstration and choral rehearsal (“Eyes on me. No pencils yet. Just watching.”). In Section 3, children begin producing the letter forms themselves (“Now it’s your turn… Magic Finger Write… Trace & Say”). Even though physical scaffolds are strong (start dots, dotted letters, choral cues), productive responsibility increases from observing to doing, marking a clear release. |
| 2 | Section 3: Guided Practice | Section 4: Application | + | Section 4 introduces a higher cognitive demand (phoneme identification + conditional write/no-write) and uses a worksheet with dotted lines, which could look like continued support. However, this is a justified cognitive-load trade-off: motor support (dotted lines) helps offset the new listening/decision requirement. Responsibility still increases because students must apply the /a/ mapping in a novel, multi-item context and decide whether to write, rather than simply trace a provided letter form on cue. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | Magic Finger Write – Uppercase A | high | No tool; teacher leads stroke-by-stroke: “Get ready… Big line up… Big line down… Little line across.”; Teacher sets start location and monitors: “Point your magic finger at the bottom line… Let me see your fingers ready… Teacher checks and gently adjusts.”; Choral, synchronous movement with teacher pacing (“Again. Ready… go…”) |
| 2 | Magic Finger Write – Lowercase a | high | Teacher assigns exact placement: “Point to the middle line where little a sits.”; Teacher cues each stroke in sequence: “Start at the middle… Rainbow around… Little line down.”; Repeated, synchronized rehearsal: “One more time. Ready… go…” |
| 3 | Trace & Say – Uppercase A (dotted template + start dot) | medium | Introduction of pencil increases productive responsibility (children create permanent marks); Strong physical scaffolds: “uppercase A with dotted lines” and “start dot”; Teacher still prompts and checks start position: “Put your pencil at the bottom where the start dot is. Let me check… Teacher scans, corrects a few.”; Choral verbal cues remain: “Write and say. Ready… go.” |
| 4 | Trace & Say – Lowercase a (dotted template + start dot) | medium | Physical scaffold remains: “dotted lowercase a” and “start dot in the middle”; Teacher checks correctness and provides criteria: “Check your little a… touches the middle and bottom…”; Children trace while saying cues, with teacher pacing: “Write and say. Ready… go.” |
| 5 | Corrective Feedback Block 1 – Starting a at the Top (reteach + try again) | high | Teacher explicitly models incorrect vs correct forms: “Watch… If I start here at the top… This does not look like our little a… This is the right way.”; Teacher directs attention and prompts metacognitive comparison: “What is different between these two?”; Teacher re-anchors start location physically: “Everyone, point to the middle line… Now trace one more little a… Say the strokes.” |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Magic Finger Write – Uppercase A | Magic Finger Write – Lowercase a | - | Support level stays similarly high. The task changes content (uppercase to lowercase formation) but teacher pacing, no-tool mode, and full verbal scripting remain constant, so there is no meaningful release. |
| 2 | Magic Finger Write – Lowercase a | Trace & Say – Uppercase A (dotted template + start dot) | + | This transition is a valid release via productive responsibility: children move from finger rehearsal (no tool, no permanent mark) to pencil tracing on paper. Although dotted lines/start dots add physical scaffolding, they offset the new fine-motor demand of pencil control (cognitive load trade-off). Overall, students take on more responsibility by producing the written marks. |
| 3 | Trace & Say – Uppercase A (dotted template + start dot) | Trace & Say – Lowercase a (dotted template + start dot) | - | Support remains about the same (same tool, same dotted-line scaffold, same choral stroke cues, similar teacher monitoring). The letter changes, but the level of scaffolding does not meaningfully fade. |
| 4 | Trace & Say – Lowercase a (dotted template + start dot) | Corrective Feedback Block 1 – Starting a at the Top (reteach + try again) | - | Support increases because the teacher re-models and tightly directs attention and the next attempt. This is appropriate error-correction (re-scaffolding) rather than continued release; therefore support did not decrease on this transition. |

**Judge Summary:** Overall, the lesson shows a clear gradual release from Section 2→3→4: (1) teacher modeling and explicit explanation of sound/gesture and letter formation, (2) guided co-practice moving from finger rehearsal to pencil tracing with strong physical/verbal scaffolds and monitoring, and (3) application where children must apply the /a/ sound-to-letter decision in a Sound-Letter Hunt with reduced moment-to-moment formation coaching. Within Section 3, there is an appropriate motor progression from Magic Finger Write to Trace & Say (a valid release via increased productive responsibility with a justified cognitive-load trade-off). The only non-decreasing step is the corrective feedback block, where support appropriately increases to address a common error (starting lowercase a at the top) before students try again. The lesson does not include later-stage fading within Section 3 (e.g., start-dot-only writing or independent writing), but it still maintains a coherent scaffold-and-release arc appropriate for PreK.

**Judge Summary:** Sections 3–4 are logically scaffolded from modeling (Section 2) to supported production (Section 3) to combined listening+writing (Section 4). The main complex Application task, Sound-Letter Hunt, is well-decomposed: children first practice initial-sound listening (Sound Detective), then explicitly learn and discriminate /a/ (gesture and /a/ vs /o/ comparison), connect /a/ to A/a, and learn letter formation with guided rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) and tracing with stroke talk (Trace & Say). No critical prerequisite component is missing for what Application actually asks (identify initial /a/ and write A/a on dotted lines; otherwise write nothing).

**Judge Summary:** Yes—multiple compare/contrast moments are embedded across the lesson. The strongest phoneme-level comparison explicitly pairs /a/ with confusable /o/ and has children produce both while attending to mouth shape (wide/flat vs rounded). The lesson also compares uppercase A vs lowercase a with explicit similarities/differences in starting point and strokes, and provides corrective feedback contrasting /m/ vs /a/ (with articulatory cues) when children write the wrong letter for /a/. An additional corrective contrast shows incorrect vs correct lowercase a formation (top start vs middle start).

Sub-metrics: Dialogue (vol-adj): 86.1% | Examples: 14 words | Responses: 98.1% | Pacing: 100.0%

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson is highly script-specific: it provides many verbatim teacher lines, explicit choral-response prompts (often with expected child responses), and frequent pacing cues (“My turn/Your turn,” “Get ready… go”). It also meets the rubric’s sound/letter requirements with mouth-shape coaching, a memory gesture for /a/, comparison to a confusable vowel (/o/), and clearly sequenced, repeatable verbal stroke cues for uppercase A and lowercase a. Stage directions appear mainly for pointing, displaying cards, monitoring, and writing models, but they do not replace the core instructional talk.

Sub-metrics: Present: 80.4% 41/51 | Specific: 87.8% 36/41 | Complete: 66.7% 34/51

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, many verbal question prompts have specific child responses (e.g., /m/, /s/, /a/, “bottom line,” “middle line,” yes/no). The biggest gaps are in (1) directives that require physical actions but have no recorded child action (e.g., pointing to big A/little a; showing magic finger readiness; pencil placement; pointing to middle line; pointing to top row), and (2) practice tasks where the script says children should BOTH do an action and say cues, but the written response is missing one component or is too generic (e.g., Magic Finger Write includes actions but not the choral stroke words; repeated “Again/one more” runs have no response; several tracing/writing prompts say “with cues” or just “children write” without specifying they write A/a and/or the exact stroke words). Strengthen these by adding explicit parentheticals that state what children DO (point, place pencil, trace) and what they SAY (the stroke sequence or sound) so the teacher can verify accuracy.

| 2 | Compare /b/ vs /p/ (voiced vs unvoiced) with vocal-box check | middle | Not listed verbatim in Form D; best estimate: adds discrimination of a confusable phoneme pair and metalinguistic feature (voicing), which is more complex than simple repetition but less complex than phoneme manipulation activities like Chop-a-Sound. |

Students must map /b/ to grapheme B/b and attend to stroke sequence/line placement orally/visually (more than simple sound repetition, but not yet pencil-writing). |
| 6 | Corrective Feedback Block #1: contrast b vs p in formation/placement (partner talk + point up/down) | middle | Error-prevention/contrast of confusable letters (b vs p) focusing on distinctive features (line extends below vs stays on lines). Comparable to a targeted discrimination/feature analysis; moderate cognitive demand. |

This also aligns with Valid Non-Escalation Pattern 2 in spirit: a planned error-prevention/feedback mini-lesson addressing a common confusion (b vs p) immediately after modeling, to improve accuracy before practice. |

**Judge Summary:** Within each section (1–4), the lesson generally moves from simpler to more complex demands or maintains an appropriate level while shifting modality (oral → manipulation → formation; finger-trace → pencil-trace; modeled application → independent application). The only apparent ‘drops’ in difficulty occur where the lesson intentionally uses (a) simpler-format practice to consolidate a just-taught discrimination/feature (gesture after b/p comparison) and (b) planned corrective feedback interruptions after observing errors during practice—both valid non-escalation patterns. No within-section sequencing violations were found.

#### Across: 100.0% (7/7 links)

| # | Earlier (Section) | Later (Section) | Builds? | Reasoning |
|---|-------------------|-----------------|---------|-----------|
| 1 | Sound Detective (beginning sound with previously learned phonemes) (Review & Connect) | Introduce /b/ (mouth/throat cues) + compare /b/ vs /p/ (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Sound Detective warms up the general routine of attending to and naming the beginning sound in a spoken word. Section 2 immediately asks children to listen closely to a new phoneme, produce it, and contrast it with a similar sound (/p/). The earlier listening/positioning routine ("listen for the first sound") supports the later, more precise discrimination task—even though the exact phoneme changes. |
| 2 | Introduce /b/ (articulation, voicing, /b/ vs /p/ contrast) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Chop-a-Sound (remove initial /b/ from CVC words) (Model, Explain & Engage) | + | Chop-a-Sound is harder than simple identification/production because children must (1) locate /b/ as the first phoneme, (2) delete it, and (3) say the remainder. The explicit voicing/articulation cues and /b/ vs /p/ comparison help stabilize the target phoneme so children can reliably "grab" and remove /b/ during deletion. |
| 3 | Chop-a-Sound (phoneme deletion of initial /b/) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound-Letter Hunt (hear beginning sound; if /b/, write b) (Application) | + | Both tasks require isolating the initial sound, but Chop-a-Sound pushes children to segment and manipulate the word around /b/ (delete it). That manipulation strengthens awareness of /b/ as a separable first sound, which supports the later decision point in Sound-Letter Hunt (does it start with /b/?) before writing the grapheme. |
| 4 | Connect /b/ sound to letter B/b + model formation with verbal stroke cues (Model, Explain & Engage) | Magic Finger Write (air/table formation with cues) (Guided Practice) | + | Teacher modeling plus shared verbal cues establishes the stroke sequence and starting points. Magic Finger Write is the next step down the gradual release: children rehearse the same formation with large motor movements (reduced fine-motor demand) while repeating the cues, directly practicing what was modeled. |
| 5 | Magic Finger Write (gross-motor rehearsal of B/b strokes) (Guided Practice) | Trace & Say (pencil tracing dotted B/b while saying cues) (Guided Practice) | + | Magic Finger Write builds motor planning and directional memory without pencil control. Trace & Say then increases difficulty by adding tool use, line adherence, and finer control, while keeping the same verbal cues—an appropriate progression from low-level to middle-level handwriting practice. |
| 6 | Corrective feedback b vs p (visual + line-placement distinction; voiced vs unvoiced reminder) (Model, Explain & Engage) | Sound-Letter Hunt (write b only when /b/ is heard) (Application) | + | Application requires choosing whether to write b at all, and then forming it correctly. The earlier b/p contrast reduces two common error types that would undermine Application: (a) phoneme confusion (/b/ vs /p/) when deciding to write, and (b) formation/placement confusion (descender for p vs on-the-lines b) when producing the letter. |
| 7 | Trace & Say (write b with correct starting point and bump placement; corrective feedback on start/bumps) (Guided Practice) | Sound-Letter Hunt (write b repeatedly in response to /b/ words) (Application) | + | Sound-Letter Hunt adds cognitive load (listen/decide) and requires independent production of b multiple times in context. Trace & Say provides the immediate handwriting competence (start at top, big line down, bump from middle) needed so that during Application children can allocate attention to sound detection rather than struggling with letter formation. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, the lesson shows a clear escalation: (1) Section 1 warms up the general routine of listening for initial sounds; (2) Section 2 introduces and stabilizes the new phoneme /b/ with articulation/voicing and a confusable contrast, then increases phonological demand with phoneme deletion (Chop-a-Sound), and explicitly links /b/ to B/b with modeled formation cues; (3) Section 3 follows an appropriate motor sequence from gross-motor rehearsal (Magic Finger Write) to fine-motor pencil tracing with verbal cues (Trace & Say) plus corrective feedback; (4) Section 4 integrates the two strands—phonemic identification of initial /b/ and writing b—via Sound-Letter Hunt. Overall, later tasks depend on and are genuinely prepared by earlier instruction, with a logical increase in difficulty and a solid gradual-release structure.

#### Release: 100.0% (3/3 transitions)

**Across-Section Support Levels**
| Section | Support Level | Indicators |
|---------|-------------|------------|
| Section 2 – Model, Explain & Engage | high | Frequent explicit teacher-first language: “My turn. Listen: /b/.”; “Watch me first.”; “My turn first. No one is using pencils yet, you are only watching.”; Teacher provides step-by-step modeling and think-alouds for new skills (voicing check, mouth position, /b/ vs /p/ comparison, Chop-a-Sound demonstration, letter formation on board).; Choral, tightly cued responding: “Now your turn. Get Ready… Go.”; “Say it with me… Ready… Go.”; Teacher supplies the full procedural/verbal scaffold for formation: “Big line down, rainbow bump, rainbow bump” and narrates line placement (“start at the top line… between the top and bottom lines”).; Teacher anticipates errors and provides explicit corrective instruction (b vs p comparison and discussion prompt). |
| Section 3 – Guided Practice | medium | Students produce the action/product with teacher leading: “We are going to trace uppercase B together… Start at the top line in the air with me.”; Ongoing verbal cues provided during performance: “Say it with me… Big line down, rainbow bump…”; Physical/visual scaffolds added for handwriting: dotted-letter worksheet; explicit start-point prompts (“Put your pencil point on the top line on the first B”).; Teacher monitoring and in-the-moment correction: “Teacher walks… corrects direction”; “Teacher walks around, gives quick support”; whole-group corrective reteach (“Everyone, eyes up here”).; Some independent attempts are embedded: “Do the next uppercase B by yourself…”; “Do one more by yourself…” |
| Section 4 – Application | medium | Task shifts to applying letter formation to a listening/spelling decision: “listen for the beginning sound, and then write the letter that matches that sound.”; Teacher provides a worked example/think-aloud first: “Let me show you. My turn… I am thinking…”; Students make the key decision each trial (is it /b/ or not) and produce the written response when appropriate: “If the word starts with /b/, you will trace and then write the letter b…”; Continued structure and prompting remains high: teacher controls pacing/boxes, asks discrimination questions (“What sound do you hear first?”), and supplies formation cueing during writing (“Say the strokes quietly as you write…”).; Motor scaffold remains (dotted lines/models on the Sound-Letter Hunt sheet): “trace or write it on the dotted lines.” |

**Across-Section Transitions**
| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 2 – Model, Explain & Engage | Section 3 – Guided Practice | + | Support decreases because responsibility shifts from teacher demonstration (“No one is using pencils yet, you are only watching”) to children producing the letterforms themselves (Magic Finger Write; then tracing). Even though Section 3 includes substantial cues and dotted templates, productive responsibility increases: children generate the movements/marks rather than only observing and repeating orally. |
| 2 | Section 3 – Guided Practice | Section 4 – Application | + | Section 4 introduces a new cognitive demand—phoneme discrimination and sound-to-letter selection in a listening task (e.g., ball vs sun)—which is more complex than isolated formation practice. Teacher keeps (or slightly increases) structure (think-aloud, box-by-box pacing) and retains dotted lines, but this functions as a cognitive-load trade-off. Students must apply learned /b/ identification plus handwriting to a new context and decide when to write vs rest, so overall responsibility meaningfully shifts toward students even with continued scaffolds. |

**Within Section 3 (Guided Practice)**
| # | Step | Support Level | Indicators |
|---|------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1 – Magic Finger Write (air/table, uppercase then lowercase) | high | Teacher leads stroke-by-stroke in unison: “trace uppercase B together… with me.”; Choral cueing and repetition: “Say it with me. Ready… Go… Again.”; No tools yet; teacher heavily directs movement and timing: “Put your magic pointer finger in the air… Start at the top line…”; Immediate monitoring/correction: “Teacher walks and quickly scans fingers; gently corrects direction if needed.” |
| 2 | Section 3 Step 2 – Trace & Say (dotted uppercase B, then dotted lowercase b; includes brief independent traces) | medium | Students create the written product with a pencil on a dotted template: “Put your pencil point… Ready to Trace & Say.”; Teacher co-performs first example: “My turn and your turn together… Ready… Go.”; Then partial release within the same activity: “Do the next uppercase B by yourself…” and “Do one more by yourself…”; Strong start-point and line-placement prompts remain: “Where do we start… (top line)”; “Put your pencil point on the top line…”; Corrective reteach occurs midstream: “Everyone, eyes up here… Let’s trace the good one in the air… Now on your paper, fix the next B…” |

| # | From | To | Released? | Reasoning |
|---|------|----|----------|-----------|
| 1 | Section 3 Step 1 – Magic Finger Write (air/table, uppercase then lowercase) | Section 3 Step 2 – Trace & Say (dotted uppercase B, then dotted lowercase b; includes brief independent traces) | + | This is a valid gradual-release move via productive responsibility: students shift from gross-motor rehearsal (finger in air/table) to producing actual written marks with a pencil. Although dotted letters add physical scaffolding (support increase), they offset the new fine-motor demand of pencil control (cognitive load trade-off). Additionally, the script includes a partial fade to independence (“Do the next… by yourself”), indicating decreased teacher control over execution. |

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 2→3→4, the lesson generally follows a gradual release arc: explicit teacher modeling and explanation in Section 2, guided co-performance and scaffolded practice in Section 3, and applied listening-to-write tasks in Section 4. The release is not a clean drop to ‘low support’ in Application because Section 4 keeps dotted writing supports and heavy teacher-led pacing/questions, but that is appropriate given the increased cognitive complexity (phoneme identification plus letter selection plus writing). Within Section 3, there is a clear motor progression from Magic Finger Write to pencil Trace & Say, with a justified scaffolding trade-off (dotted templates compensate for added pencil demands) and small embedded independent attempts that move responsibility toward students.

**Judge Summary:** Sections 3–4 show generally strong scaffolding. Letter-formation demands in Guided Practice are preceded by explicit modeling of stroke sequence, start points, and line placement in Section 2, then further decomposed via Magic Finger Write before pencil tracing in Trace & Say. The Application task (Sound-Letter Hunt) requires initial-sound identification, /b/ discrimination (especially vs /p/), sound-to-letter mapping, and the motor plan to write lowercase b; all of these are explicitly taught earlier (Sections 1–3). The lesson also includes a worked example in Section 4 that teaches the combined 'listen → identify first sound → choose letter → write' routine before children perform it. No critical component skill appears missing for the stated Application demand (writing b for words beginning with /b/).

One notable instance is in Guided Practice (Trace & Say, uppercase B start point) where the first response (“top line”) is followed mostly by confirmation plus directions, without an explicit why. However, the very next elicited response in that same mini-sequence is followed by a clear explanation (“Because we started at the top line… and made two bumps…”), meeting the criterion of explaining after the first and/or second response.

**Judge Summary:** Yes—multiple explicit compare/contrast opportunities are embedded in the lesson. The lesson directly compares the target phoneme /b/ with the confusable /p/ using articulatory/voicing cues (lips together for both; buzzing/voiced vs quiet/voiceless) and has children produce both sounds with hands on their “vocal box.” It also compares letter forms b vs p in corrective feedback, highlighting similarities (both have a line + bump) and differences in formation/placement (p descends below the bottom line; b stays on the lines, bump orientation), with students actively saying/gesturing for both.

**Judge Summary:** Overall, Sections 1–4 are highly script-specific and align well to Rubric #10: they include explicit pronunciation coaching (mouth position, voicing/buzzing with hand on throat), a memory gesture (+Gesture bouncing ball), and sequenced verbal cues for letter formation (uppercase B: “Big line down, rainbow bump, rainbow bump”; lowercase b: “Big line down, rainbow bump from the middle”). Most instructional moves are supported with verbatim teacher talk and frequent pacing cues (“My turn/Your turn,” “Ready… Go,” “Hands down”), plus multiple opportunities for students to give specific responses (e.g., children say /b/, /p/, ‘at’, ‘ug’). The main places requiring teacher improvisation are the bracketed/brace directions (e.g., choosing 3–4 known sound cards; “Teacher quickly does 2 more review items”; circulation/monitoring). Also, Section 1 could be more fully scripted by naming the additional two review items (specific words/cards) rather than leaving them generic.

**Judge Summary:** All four scripted sections (1–4) reference at least one Form D activity by name. Materials fully aligns with the named activities used; no activity used in the lesson is missing from Materials. Some non-activity routines in Section 2 (sound articulation/voicing comparison and letter formation modeling) are generic and not labeled as Form D activities, but they do not replace the named Chop-a-Sound segment.

Sub-metrics: Present: 93.9% 46/49 | Specific: 63.0% 29/46 | Complete: 63.3% 31/49

**Judge Summary:** Across Sections 1–4, many interaction points include a child response, but several are missing either (a) any child response for directives (e.g., ‘Ready…Go’ to start Sound Detective; ‘Pick up your pencil and show me’; ‘Hands down’), or (b) the required verbal/action component for practice routines. The most common gap is practice tasks where children should BOTH do an action (hand on throat, air-trace, write/trace) AND say the cue/sound; the script often captures only one component (often just the sound, or just the tracing/writing). Several discussion/question responses are also somewhat non-specific due to vague wording like ‘or similar,’ ‘etc.,’ or ‘children talk briefly,’ which reduces checkability.
